Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template
• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to
annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in
the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all
relevant documents.
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60.
Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard
time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 10.
• Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy, project and programme funding proposals will be
disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the
redaction of any information that may n be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are
asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.
Please submit the completed proposal to:
fundingproposal@gcfund.org

A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
Prolonged droughts across the country have accelerated vegetation loss in desert environments, reducing ecosystem
services related to sediment stabilisation and water infiltration. As a result, the rate of dune advancement along the
Sahara-Sahel boundary has increased and greater sand encroachment has been observed in oases, with subsequent
impacts on water resources, agricultural productivity and income generation across northern Mauritania.
Siltation of dams, boreholes, wells and rivers has reduced the accessibility and availability of already scarce water
resources in the region. Moreover, reduced rainfall and water infiltration resulting from vegetation loss have increased
the depth of the water table. This has led to many pastoral wells drying up, impacting pastoralists’ abilities to maintain
nomadic lifestyles. Consequently, many nomadic pastoralists are adopting sedentary lifestyles around rural oases,
increasing pressure on limited natural resources such as the demand for food, water and income to support rural-urban
communities growth. Sand inundation is threatening important socio-economic infrastructure such as roads, schools and
hospitals located within the Sahel-Sahara region. Indeed, in extreme cases, sand encroachment has resulted in the
displacement of entire villages and communities. These processes will continue to worsen, with the Sahara Desert
expected to spread southwards into the country's most fertile lands in the Sahelian region and near the Senegal River.
To slow the rate of desertification and enhance water security in Mauritania, the restoration and conservation of
ecosystems along the Sahel-Saharan boundary is required to enhance ecosystem services relating to dune stabilisation,
water infiltration and the provision of natural resources. Four regional hubs, where desertification processes are most
severe and adaptation needs are greatest, have been identified in northern Mauritania. These hubs include: i) Aoujeft,
in the Adrar province; ii) Rachid, in the Tagant province; iii) Tamcheket, in the Hodh El Gharbi province; and iv) Nema,
in the Hodh El Chargui province.
2 The total lifespan of the project/programme is defined as the maximum number of years over which the outcomes of the investment are
expected to be effective. This is different from the project/programme implementation period.
3 Refer to the Policy of Co-financing of the GCF.

B
2. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION
B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
Climate change is profoundly impacting Mauritanian communities, particularly those living along the Sahel-Saharan
boundary that extends from the northwest to the southeast of the country. A noticeable impact of climate change-
induced desertification is the accelerated advance of sand dunes. Dams, boreholes, wells and rivers are becoming
increasingly silted as a result of sand inundation, reducing access to and the availability of already-strained water
resources (see Figure 60 in Annex 2). Arable land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, severely compromising
the agricultural (see Figure 27 in Annex 2) and livestock practices in the oases. In addition, sand inundation threatens
critical socio-economic infrastructure, including, inter alia, roads, school buildings and hospitals. In extreme cases, sand
dunes have displaced entire villages, in some cases more than once (see Figure 15 in Annex 2). The ongoing decline
in the water table has also dried up wells commonly used by pastoralist herders, impacting their ability to practice
transhumance4. Combined with the climate change-induced loss of rangelands and pastures, water scarcity has
contributed to previously nomadic herders becoming more sedentary around rural oases where resource access is
reliable. However, sedentarisation increases pressure on limited resources, creating a source of conflict — particularly
between farmers and herders.
Four regional hubs have been identified along the boundary where desertification processes are most severe and the
need for adaptation is greatest. These hubs are: i) Aoujeft, in the wilayah of Adrar; ii) Rachid, in the wilyah of Tagant;
iii) Tamcheket, in the wilayah of Hodh El Gharbi; and iv) Nema, in the wilayah of Hodh El Chargui. Each hub covers
an area of 80–85 km2, spans several communes and comprises an urban centre surrounded by rural hinterland. The
locations of the four hubs — Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema. Aoujeft hub comprises six communes with a total
population of ~35,600; Rachid hub spans three communes and sustains ~25,600 people; Tamcheket hub covers five
communes and houses ~43,300 inhabitants; and Nema hub encompasses ten communes with a total population of
~99,6005.
Climate science basis6
Observed climate change
Observations of historical climate trends show that the mean annual temperature in Mauritania has increased by 0.3°C
per decade over the last 70 years — from 26.10°C in 1950 to 28.46°C in 20207. Although warming has been observed
across all major climatic zones in the country, temperature increases have been greatest over the Sahara Desert and
lowest over the Senegal River Headwaters (Error! Reference source not found.)8. Moreover, the latest available 30-
year weather station data (1981–2010) indicate that, in general, temperatures have also increased within the focal hubs
of Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema (see Table 27 in Annex 2 for observed temperature changes in the four focal
hubs)9.
In addition to shifting temperatures, climate change in Mauritania has induced: i) an increase in the frequency of extreme
rainfall events and associated flash floods; and ii) a general decline in low-intensity, long-duration precipitation events
(see Table 32 in Annex 2 for observed precipitation trends in the four focal hubs). Consequently, while heavy
precipitation events have become more intense, rainfall is increasingly concentrated over shorter time periods. This
shift has resulted in increased rainfall irregularity and prolonged dry spells across the region10. Precipitation has
decreased during the wet season and over the entire year in Nema, Tamcheket and Rachid. In contrast, the Aoujeft
hub has undergone a small but statistically significant increase in its overall precipitation. Despite the overall decline in
average precipitation, an increase in extreme daily rainfall, which often results in flash floods, has been observed in all
four hubs over both time frames.
4 The action or practice of moving livestock from one grazing ground to another in a seasonal cycle, typically to lowlands in winter and
highlands in summer.
5Annex 2 — Appendix 4: Climate Change Risk Assessment
6 Detailed primary and secondary climate analyses for the proposed project are presented in Annex 2: Feasibility Study, in Section 3 and
Appendices 3 and 4, respectively. The information presented below is a summary of what is presented in the more detailed analyses.
While detailed climate analyses in the Feasibility Study were conducted using CMIP 5 data, the findings presented here are also
consistent with more recent CMIP 6 model findings.
7 World Bank. 2022. Climate change knowledge portal: Mauritania. Available at:
https://climateknowledgeportal.worldbank.org/country/mauritania/trends-variability-historical.
8 AfDB. 2018. National climate change profile. Available at: https://www.afdb.org/en/documents/mauritania-national-climate-change-profile.
9 Annex 2 - Appendix 2: Trend Analysis Report for Mauritania.
10 AfDB. 2018. National climate change profile. Available at: https://www.afdb.org/en/documents/mauritania-national-climate-change-profile.

=== PAGE 8 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 4
B
Observed potential evapotranspiration (ETP) values are high in all four hubs (1,700–2,000 mm/year), with the highest
values observed in Nema and Tamcheket. In addition, each hub has negative differences between precipitation and
evapotranspiration (P-ETP), indicating that water loss via ETP exceeds water gained via precipitation.
Sand movements are driven by the prevalence of strong winds from the north of Mauritania including: i) the north-
eastern trade winds; and ii) the harmattan — a dry wind strongest from late November to mid-March. Together, these
winds carry large amounts of dust and sand across the Mauritanian territory.
Projected climate change
All four target hubs are projected to experience an increase in both minimum and maximum temperatures under future
RCP4.5 (intermediate) and RCP8.5 (high) greenhouse gas emissions scenarios, with larger temperature increases
projected under RCP8.5 than RCP4.5. These projected changes are reflected in Table 31, Annex 2. In line with
increasing temperatures, the frequency of heat waves is predicted to increase across all four target hubs by 2050 under
both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios11. Projected changes in the number of days with heatwaves per
decade are presented in Table 42 in Annex 2.
The projected evolution of precipitation trends varies among hubs. For example, no significant change in precipitation
is projected in Aoujeft by 2050 under the RCP4.5 GHG emissions scenario. In contrast, annual precipitation is expected
to significantly decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema, relative to the reference period (1981–2010). Similarly, under
the RCP8.5 emissions scenario, mean annual precipitation in Aoujeft is not projected to change significantly relative to
the reference period. However, by 2050, mean annual precipitation is expected to decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and
Nema. Projected changes in precipitation within the four target hubs are presented in Table 35 in Annex 2.
In line with increasing hub-level temperature trends and decreasing precipitation trends, potential evapotranspiration
(ETP) is projected to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios
(Table 40 in Annex 2). As a result of increased ETP and limited rainfall, the water balance deficit (P-ETP) in all four
hubs is also projected to increase significantly (p<0.1) by 2050, as shown in Table 41, Annex 212.
The combination of projected temperature rise, ETP and declining rainfall will increase the extent and severity of
droughts at national and local levels. By 2040, the median number of consecutive dry days (CDD) for Mauritania is
predicted to be between 219 and 230, compared to the historical value of 216 days in 1995. Moreover, by 2100, annual
CCD is expected to reach between 225 and 236 days, representing a change of between 9 and 20 days relative to
1995. Another indicator of future drought conditions — the Standardised Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index
(SPEI)13 — displays a clear trend toward worsening drought conditions in Mauritania. All Shared Socioeconomic
Pathways (SSP)14 scenarios predict that SPEI values will fall below zero in the future, indicating an increase in dry
years relative to the historical average. Moreover, by 2100, 90th percentile values are expected to fall below negative
two, signifying an increase in extremely dry years.
Although data regarding projected changes in wind regimes across the country are limited, a recent global review
indicates that near-surface wind speeds are projected to decrease under future global warming scenarios (1.5–4°C
above pre-industrial levels) over the parts of the Northern Hemisphere, including Mauritania15. At the hub level, models
projecting changes in wind speed and direction under future climate change scenarios are currently unavailable.
Consequently, the assumption that wind conditions will remain constant under future climate scenarios has been applied
to all four hubs. The combination of constant windy conditions and a generally warmer and drier climate is likely to
facilitate the southward expansion of the Sahara into the Sahelian region of the country. This trend will further impact
the communities of the project’s focal hubs.

Observed potential evapotranspiration (ETP) values are high in all four hubs (1,700–2,000 mm/year), with the highest
values observed in Nema and Tamcheket. In addition, each hub has negative differences between precipitation and
evapotranspiration (P-ETP), indicating that water loss via ETP exceeds water gained via precipitation.
Sand movements are driven by the prevalence of strong winds from the north of Mauritania including: i) the north-
eastern trade winds; and ii) the harmattan — a dry wind strongest from late November to mid-March. Together, these
winds carry large amounts of dust and sand across the Mauritanian territory.
Projected climate change
All four target hubs are projected to experience an increase in both minimum and maximum temperatures under future
RCP4.5 (intermediate) and RCP8.5 (high) greenhouse gas emissions scenarios, with larger temperature increases
projected under RCP8.5 than RCP4.5. These projected changes are reflected in Table 31, Annex 2. In line with
increasing temperatures, the frequency of heat waves is predicted to increase across all four target hubs by 2050 under
both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios11. Projected changes in the number of days with heatwaves per
decade are presented in Table 42 in Annex 2.
The projected evolution of precipitation trends varies among hubs. For example, no significant change in precipitation
is projected in Aoujeft by 2050 under the RCP4.5 GHG emissions scenario. In contrast, annual precipitation is expected
to significantly decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema, relative to the reference period (1981–2010). Similarly, under
the RCP8.5 emissions scenario, mean annual precipitation in Aoujeft is not projected to change significantly relative to
the reference period. However, by 2050, mean annual precipitation is expected to decrease in Rachid, Tamcheket and
Nema. Projected changes in precipitation within the four target hubs are presented in Table 35 in Annex 2.
In line with increasing hub-level temperature trends and decreasing precipitation trends, potential evapotranspiration
(ETP) is projected to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both the RCP4.5 and RCP8.5 emissions scenarios
(Table 40 in Annex 2). As a result of increased ETP and limited rainfall, the water balance deficit (P-ETP) in all four
hubs is also projected to increase significantly (p<0.1) by 2050, as shown in Table 41, Annex 212.
The combination of projected temperature rise, ETP and declining rainfall will increase the extent and severity of
droughts at national and local levels. By 2040, the median number of consecutive dry days (CDD) for Mauritania is
predicted to be between 219 and 230, compared to the historical value of 216 days in 1995. Moreover, by 2100, annual
CCD is expected to reach between 225 and 236 days, representing a change of between 9 and 20 days relative to
1995. Another indicator of future drought conditions — the Standardised Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index
(SPEI)13 — displays a clear trend toward worsening drought conditions in Mauritania. All Shared Socioeconomic
Pathways (SSP)14 scenarios predict that SPEI values will fall below zero in the future, indicating an increase in dry
years relative to the historical average. Moreover, by 2100, 90th percentile values are expected to fall below negative
two, signifying an increase in extremely dry years.
Although data regarding projected changes in wind regimes across the country are limited, a recent global review
indicates that near-surface wind speeds are projected to decrease under future global warming scenarios (1.5–4°C
above pre-industrial levels) over the parts of the Northern Hemisphere, including Mauritania15. At the hub level, models
projecting changes in wind speed and direction under future climate change scenarios are currently unavailable.
Consequently, the assumption that wind conditions will remain constant under future climate scenarios has been applied
to all four hubs. The combination of constant windy conditions and a generally warmer and drier climate is likely to
facilitate the southward expansion of the Sahara into the Sahelian region of the country. This trend will further impact
the communities of the project’s focal hubs.

B
Problem statement
Climate change will continue to exacerbate the drivers of desertification in Mauritania, threatening the structural integrity
of the fragile oases that underpin the natural resource-based livelihoods of rural communities. Drivers of desertification
include changes in the spatial and temporal patterns of rainfall and increases in temperatures and ETP that, when
combined, lead to: i) more frequent, severe and prolonged drought events16; ii) reduced water security; iii) more intense
desertification processes; and iv) siltation of dams, boreholes, wells and rivers, which reduces the accessibility and
availability of already strained water resources v) the drying up of wells commonly used by pastoralist herders.. Of
these impacts, the increasing frequency, severity and time span of droughts are particularly damaging, as they already
contribute to vegetation loss, promoting dune advancement and accelerated sand encroachment in fragile oases (see
Figure 140 in Annex 2).
Without urgent intervention, these processes will worsen in the future. The Sahara Desert is expected to spread south
into the country’s most fertile lands in the Sahelian region. Rural communities in Mauritania have limited financial and
coordination capacity to slow the rate of desertification along the Sahel-Saharan boundary and improve water security.
Preferred adaptation solution
An ecosystem-based adaptation approach within the target hubs is the preferred option for responding to the impacts
of climate changed-induced desertification within the four target hubs because it is cost-effective, easily implemented
and has the potential to provide valuable ecosystem goods and services to climate-vulnerable communities dependent
on the oases (for an example, see Figure 145 in Annex 2). This approach includes establishing 2,123 ha of green-grey
dune fixation infrastructure and 120 km of protective fence line to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of
degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation and the supply of natural
resources.
To support the adaptive capacity of the communities in the target hubs, the proposed project will develop or strengthen
sustainable agriculture-based livelihoods. By providing an alternative source of income to current extraction-based
livelihoods, these livelihood options will improve the sustainability of green-grey infrastructure approaches by mitigating
the risk of communities resorting to maladaptive coping mechanisms.
Barriers to the preferred solution
Barrier 1: Limited coordination and technical capacity to implement adaptation interventions at the community level
Implementing entities, as well as government and non-governmental stakeholders in Mauritania, have limited
institutional capacities and incentive to coordinate efforts to address climate risks, which has restricted CCA efforts in
the four target hubs. Government officials at national-, regional- and local levels, as well as staff from crucial sectors at
the local level, have limited technical capacity to develop and implement adaptation initiatives. Local authorities and
stakeholders at the forefront of managing climate change impacts, such as community leaders, often have inadequate
training on incorporating climate change considerations into decision-making or designing and implementing climate-
resilient natural resource management and land-use planning.
Barrier 2: Limited financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient land and natural resources management at the
community level
Most urban centres in Mauritania are characterised by predominantly informal trade, limited infrastructure and public-
service coverage, high levels of self-employment and low human capital. These characteristics generally discourage
private-sector investment in sustainable development and CCA17. Although the Government of the Islamic Republic of
Mauritania (GoM) has introduced a number of regulatory and legislative reforms in an attempt to boost the private sector
by 2025 — for example, through the establishment of the Higher Council for Investment in 2020 — several obstacles
persist, impeding local and foreign investment in sustainable development. These obstacles include: i) difficulty in
accessing microfinance for local business development; ii) limited market access within rural and semi-urban areas; iii)
low levels of technical and professional training; iv) limited governance within the financial services sector; and v) limited
private-sector organisation18. Consequently, past projects have been unable to mobilise substantial private-sector
investment in CCA. For this reason, the proposed project will leverage public-sector funding to co-finance adaptation
interventions.
16 AfDB. 2018. National climate change profile. Available at: https://www.afdb.org/en/documents/mauritania-national-climate-change-
profile.v
17 Centre for Affordable Housing Finance in Africa. 2018. Africa Housing Finance Yearbook 2018 — Mauritania. Available at:
http://housingfinanceafrica.org/app/uploads/Mauritania-2018-Final.pdf
18 Arab NGO Network for Development (ANND). 2022. The Private Sector and Sustainable Development in Mauritania. Available at:

=== PAGE 10 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6
B
Barrier 3: Limited knowledge of the climate change impacts, risks, vulnerabilities and adaptation options across the four
target hubs
Communities in rural areas — particularly farmers and pastoralists — are not well informed about current and expected
climate change impacts, risks and vulnerabilities related to water availability, agricultural production and other land-use
practices. Moreover, the target communities have limited technical knowledge on adapting to these changes and
increasing climate resilience. To date, attempts to stabilise dunes in the four target hubs have rarely been consistent,
strategic or integrated into comprehensive plans. The NAP Knowledge Management Platform is still under development,
as a result, national-level stakeholders seldom pass on information to regional or local levels. There is limited inter-
sectoral collaboration and knowledge-sharing between ministries, departments and stakeholders.
Barrier 4: Limited integration of climate change adaptation into land-use planning and natural-resource management
strategies at the regional and local levels
Although urban plans developed for Nema and Rachid incorporate some measures for climate-risk reduction,
mainstreaming adaptation and mitigation strategies into plans at the regional and local levels has largely been
overlooked. In the past, failure to recognise the importance of a participatory approach has resulted in weak coordination
between climate change-related projects and associated activities within the target region19,20. While some basic water-
management plans exist within the target region, pasture- and rangeland-management plans that consider climate
change are notably absent in the target hubs21.
Project strategy for overcoming barriers to adaptation and achieving a paradigm shift in Mauritania
The goal of the proposed project is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems in northern Mauritania
against the impacts of climate change. This will be achieved by investing in land rehabilitation and sand-dune fixation,
water resources management, climate-resilient agriculture and livelihood diversification in four rural-urban hubs: Aoujeft
(in the Adrar wilayah), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamcheket (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and Nema (in the
Hodh El Chargui wilayah). To ensure the success of this approach, the project will focus on two main outcomes:
• Outcome 1. Increased implementation and upscaling of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures across the
Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.; and
• Outcome 2. Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania have increased
livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water access, and sustainable land- and
natural resources management practices.
Output 1.1 will address all four of the identified barriers to adaptation by establishing coordination platforms to improve
technical capacity, facilitate knowledge sharing on natural resource management and sustainable land-use planning
and the implementation of EbA activities at the regional and commune level. By creating and disseminating knowledge
on EbA, Output 1.2 will contribute to addressing barrier 1 and barrier 3.
Under Output 2.1, ~2,123 ha of green-grey infrastructure will be established across the four target hubs to facilitate the
rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation
and the supply of natural resources. Co-developing commune-level rehabilitation plans will support the integration of
climate change adaptation into land-use planning and natural-resource management strategies at the regional and
local levels. Investment in climate-resilient land management will contribute to overcoming barriers 2 and 3.
Under Output 2.2, community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) will be established. Commune-level water
monitoring and regulation plans will be developed, and physical water management infrastructure and water access
points will be established along historical transhumance routes. This will contribute to overcoming barriers 2, 3 and 4.
Output 2.3 will facilitate the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and sustainable diversified livelihoods for
farmers and community members within the target communes. Farmers will be trained by MEDD and MDR extension
officers and representatives from commune-level technical committees (CTCs) to practice climate-resilient crop
agriculture and to use improved agricultural technologies. These extension officers will also develop seed banks and
provide climate-resilient seed varietals to newly establishing nurseries — for distribution to 500 farmers and sale
19 Food and Agriculture Organisation. 2010. Fighting sand encroachment: Lessons from Mauritania. Available at:
https://www.fao.org/3/i1488e/i1488e.pdf
20 World Bank. 1994. Mauritania Country Environmental Strategy Paper. Available at:
https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/537131468756600019/pdf/multi-page.pdf
21 The Adaptation Partnership. 2011. Review of current and planned adaptation action — Mauritania. Available at:
https://www.preventionweb.net/publication/mauritania-review-current-and-planned-adaptation-action

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7
B
thereafter — and training to nursery operators, to support land rehabilitation, dune fixation and horticulture activities.
As some nurseries already exist within the project’s target regions, there is an opportunity for upscaling as well as the
establishment of new nurseries and market gardens, encouraging livelihood diversification and empowering women in
agriculture. In addition, an on-granting mechanism will promote and support community-led initiatives, fostering the
autonomous uptake of EbA practices by community members, thereby increasing community ownership and the
sustainability of project activities beyond the project implementation timeline.
The theory of change (ToC) states that If an ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach is used to restore
ecosystems, fix dunes in place and sustainably manage natural resources along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in
Mauritania, supported by transformational change in policies, planning, knowledge management and capacity-building,
then the resilience of communities and ecosystems to the impacts of climate change-induced desertification —
including sand inundation, water scarcity, rangeland degradation and reduced agricultural productivity — will be
strengthened because the provision of ecosystem services will be enhanced, communities in rural-urban hubs will
have strengthened livelihood security and sub-national decision-makers will have strengthened capacities to invest in-
and implement EbA measures. This resilience will be enhanced particularly if supported by transformational change in
policies, planning, knowledge management and capacity-building. As ecosystem services are enhanced, livelihood
security for communities in rural-urban hubs will be enhanced. Moreover, sub-national decision-makers’ capacity to
invest in and implement EbA measures will be strengthened. This, in turn, will support a paradigm shift towards effective
CCA in Mauritania.
The assumptions underpinning the success of the proposed project are that: i) target communities and the government
will continue to engage in the development and maintenance of adaptation interventions; ii) climate change knowledge
generated will be used to inform effective management practices in the long term; iii) sustainable livelihoods will provide
sufficient incentive to prevent the continuation of unsustainable land-management practices; and iv) improving market
access will increase adoption of sustainable agriculture practices.
The ToC is provided in Figure 1 below.

Outcome 1 ☐ ☐ ☐ ☐ ☒ ☐ ☐ ☒
Outcome 2 ☐ ☐ ☐ ☐ ☒ ☒ ☒ ☒
The selection of ARA1, ARA2, ARA3 and ARA4 is based on the direct and measurable contribution of the project
outcomes and outputs to enhancing community and ecosystem resilience in the target hubs. The proposed project
adopts an integrated ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach that addresses environmental and socio-economic
vulnerabilities caused by desertification and climate change.
Outcome 1
• ARA1: Under Outcome 1, the project will strengthen governance and technical capacity for climate adaptation by
establishing local coordination structures and integrating climate change considerations into sub-national plans and
budgets. The involvement of community representatives in these structures will enhance community ownership of
adaptation measures, ensuring that vulnerable groups have a platform to influence decision-making and benefit
directly from resilience-building interventions.
• ARA4: The strengthened governance and knowledge-sharing mechanisms established under Outcome 1 will
improve the planning and management of ecosystem restoration activities. By mainstreaming ecosystem-based
adaptation into local plans and enhancing coordination among stakeholders, the project will promote sustainable
land-use practices and strengthen the long-term resilience of ecosystems.
Outcome 2
• ARA1: Under Outcome 2, the project will improve the resilience of vulnerable communities by reducing exposure
to desertification and sand encroachment through the establishment of green-grey infrastructure. Additionally,
training and technical support for climate-resilient agriculture, sustainable livestock management and alternative
livelihoods will enable communities to diversify income sources and reduce dependency on fragile ecosystems.
• ARA2: Under Outcome 2, the project will enhance water and food security by establishing water management
infrastructure, installing improved irrigation systems and promoting sustainable water management practices.
Increased access to water for agricultural and household use will improve community health and well-being while
climate-resilient agricultural practices will strengthen food security.
• ARA3: Under Outcome 2, the project will increase the resilience of infrastructure by supporting the establishment
of green-grey dune fixation infrastructure and protective greenbelts. These measures will protect houses, roads
and other critical infrastructure from sand encroachment, thereby reducing long-term maintenance costs,
increasing the longevity of built infrastructure and mitigating displacement resulting from sand encroachment on
infrastructure.
• ARA4: Under Outcome 2, the project will restore and protect ecosystems by establishing green-grey dune fixation
infrastructure and improving natural resource management. Increased ecosystem health will enhance the
provision of ecosystem services such as soil stabilisation, groundwater recharge and improved biodiversity,
strengthening the overall resilience of both ecosystems and communities.
This alignment demonstrates how the project’s integrated approach will generate social, economic and environmental
adaptation benefits, directly supporting the GCF's strategic objectives. Further information regarding the proposed
project’s contribution to the GCF results areas is provided in Section D.1.

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)
The objective of the proposed project is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems22 against climate
change-induced desertification in the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania. The project will focus on four rural-urban
hubs: Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamcheket (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and
Nema (in the Hodh El Chargui wilayah). For a more detailed description of the proposed project’s Outcome, Outputs
and activities, please refer to Section 11 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.
Outcome 1. Increased implementation and upscaling of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures across the
Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.
Outcome 1 will contribute to creating an enabling environment for climate change and gender-responsive land-use
planning and natural resource management at the sub-national level in Mauritania. This will enhance the long-term
sustainability and scalability of climate resilience-building interventions across the Sahara-Sahel boundary.
Output 1.1 will strengthen governance structures to support the implementation, maintenance and upscaling of EbA
measures across the four target hubs and the integration of climate change considerations and Ecosystem-based
Adaptation (EbA) into government plans, policies and budgets. This will be achieved by establishing and training
commune-level technical committees (CTCs) in 12 priority communes across Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema
(process for selection of priority communes is detailed in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study), to facilitate natural
resource management, sustainable land-use planning, EbA implementation and knowledge sharing at regional and
local levels. Each CTC will be chaired by the respective commune's mayor, with members comprising DREDD
representatives; 1-2 members of the Communal Council, representatives of Associations for Natural Resources
(AGLCs) and of Association of Oasis Producers’ Groups (AOPGs); civil society representatives; and local community
members. The CTCs will be trained by extension officers from the MEDD and DREDDs to supervise, manage and
upscale CCA measures introduced under Output 2.1, Output 2.2 and Output 2.3 of the proposed project. The CTCs will
also coordinate knowledge-sharing between local community members, commune-level authorities (mayors) and
regional authorities (DREDDs and walis).
Output 1.2 will focus on developing and disseminating knowledge products that support informed decision-making for
climate change adaptation and the upscaling of the project approach and best practices. Knowledge products generated
under the project will include training manuals, implementation guides, lessons learnt and best practices identified by
both experts and local stakeholders, and monitoring and evaluation reports. These knowledge products will be
disseminated to stakeholders across the four wilayahs (including non-target communes) using the NAP Knowledge
Management Platform, which is currently under development by the NAP readiness project. The project will also support
the NAP Knowledge Management Platform to better reach local-level end users, by packaging knowledge in formats
that are accessible to sub-national decision-makers and communities.
22 Table 74 in Annex 2 shows how the project aligns with the GCF Ecosystem and Ecosystem Services Sectoral Guide (2022).

B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | 
The objective of the proposed project is to enhance the resilience of communities and ecosystems22 against climate
change-induced desertification in the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania. The project will focus on four rural-urban
hubs: Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamcheket (in the Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and
Nema (in the Hodh El Chargui wilayah). For a more detailed description of the proposed project’s Outcome, Outputs
and activities, please refer to Section 11 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.
Outcome 1. Increased implementation and upscaling of Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures across the
Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.
Outcome 1 will contribute to creating an enabling environment for climate change and gender-responsive land-use
planning and natural resource management at the sub-national level in Mauritania. This will enhance the long-term
sustainability and scalability of climate resilience-building interventions across the Sahara-Sahel boundary.
Output 1.1 will strengthen governance structures to support the implementation, maintenance and upscaling of EbA
measures across the four target hubs and the integration of climate change considerations and Ecosystem-based
Adaptation (EbA) into government plans, policies and budgets. This will be achieved by establishing and training
commune-level technical committees (CTCs) in 12 priority communes across Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema
(process for selection of priority communes is detailed in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study), to facilitate natural
resource management, sustainable land-use planning, EbA implementation and knowledge sharing at regional and
local levels. Each CTC will be chaired by the respective commune's mayor, with members comprising DREDD
representatives; 1-2 members of the Communal Council, representatives of Associations for Natural Resources
(AGLCs) and of Association of Oasis Producers’ Groups (AOPGs); civil society representatives; and local community
members. The CTCs will be trained by extension officers from the MEDD and DREDDs to supervise, manage and
upscale CCA measures introduced under Output 2.1, Output 2.2 and Output 2.3 of the proposed project. The CTCs will
also coordinate knowledge-sharing between local community members, commune-level authorities (mayors) and
regional authorities (DREDDs and walis).
Output 1.2 will focus on developing and disseminating knowledge products that support informed decision-making for
climate change adaptation and the upscaling of the project approach and best practices. Knowledge products generated
under the project will include training manuals, implementation guides, lessons learnt and best practices identified by
both experts and local stakeholders, and monitoring and evaluation reports. These knowledge products will be
disseminated to stakeholders across the four wilayahs (including non-target communes) using the NAP Knowledge
Management Platform, which is currently under development by the NAP readiness project. The project will also support
the NAP Knowledge Management Platform to better reach local-level end users, by packaging knowledge in formats
that are accessible to sub-national decision-makers and communities. |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

Output 1.1. Governance structures are strengthened to support the implementation of EbA measures and the
integration of climate change considerations and EbA into government plans, policies and budgets.
Activity 1.1.1. Establish coordination platforms and train participants to facilitate knowledge sharing, natural-resource
management, sustainable land-use planning and the implementation of EbA activities at regional and local levels.
Under Activity 1.1.1, the proposed GCF project will:
• establish 12 commune-level technical committees (CTCs) across the four target hubs;
• enhance capacity for coordination and knowledge-sharing between local and regional stakeholders within the target
hubs; and
• support the integration of climate change considerations and EbA into sub-national plans, policies and budgets.
To maximise the impact potential project activities, given the finite nature of financial resources and human capital23,
CTCs will be established within the 12 priority communes targeted by the project (i.e., most vulnerable and exposed),
with the view to upscale project interventions to additional communes in the future.
Table 1. The 12 priority communes for the proposed project

Representatives of the CTCs will coordinate with DREDD directorates in each target wilayah to oversee the following
mandates:
• enhance cross-sectoral coordination for CCA between national, regional and local stakeholders;
• ensure that target communities' needs and concerns are considered when developing and implementing natural-
resource management and sustainable land-use plans and policies;
• facilitate two-way information- and knowledge-sharing between local-level stakeholders and regional authorities24;
• support the training of community members to implement and maintain adaptation interventions introduced under
proposed the project;
• support the integration of climate change considerations into regional- and commune-level policies, plans and
budgets for natural-resources management and land-use planning; and
• develop the technical and institutional capacities of relevant stakeholders to drive scalable and sustainable climate
action.
In accordance with Mauritanian law, the 12 CTCs will be formally established via announcement; however, over the
course of the project, their legitimacy in the relevant legislature will be pursued via registration with the Ministry of the
Interior. Formalisation of these project structures will maintain their medium- to long-term sustainability and facilitate
community ownership of project activities beyond the project implementation period.
Each CTC will be chaired by the mayor of its respective commune and its members will include, inter alia,
representatives from the regional environmental delegation (DREDD), 1-2 members of the Communal Council, civil
society representatives, and community members. Existing community structures and systems — such as already
established Associations for Natural Resources (AGLCs) — will be leveraged, where applicable, to accelerate the
establishment of the CTCs and support their sustainability.
Sub-activities implemented to deliver this activity are:
• Sub-activity 1.1.1.1. Establish commune-level technical committees (CTCs) within each priority commune in the
four target hubs.
• Sub-activity 1.1.1.2. Deliver training workshops to CTCs, enhancing members' capacities to implement and
manage project activities, support the integration of climate change in regional- and commune-level policies, plans
and budgets, and facilitate knowledge sharing between regional- and local-level stakeholders.

Output 1.2. Knowledge products developed and disseminated to support decision making and upscaling.
Activity 1.2.1. Develop knowledge products on project lessons learned and best practices through a participatory
process engaging the communities.
To ensure that project interventions are responsive to local adaptation needs and generate lasting benefits, Activity
1.2.1 will focus on systematically capturing community perspectives, lessons learned and best practices that emerge
throughout project implementation. By integrating participatory approaches, the project will facilitate knowledge
exchange between local stakeholders and the Project Management Unit (PMU), enabling adaptive management and
the dissemination of successful adaptation strategies that can be referenced when upscaling or replicating climate
change adaptation strategies across Mauritania, or when developing new climate change adaptation projects.
Under this activity, bi-annual gender-inclusive discussions will be held between the PMU, the CTCs and communities
in the target hubs. These discussions will provide a structured forum for community members to share their experiences,
voice concerns and highlight successes and challenges related to project interventions. Ensuring gender inclusivity in
these engagements will help capture diverse perspectives, particularly from marginalised groups that may otherwise
be excluded from decision-making processes. The insights gained from these discussions will be synthesised into
community engagement reports, which will serve as a record of local experiences and provide valuable feedback for
refining project implementation strategies.
In parallel, the project will undertake a systematic identification of best practices and lessons learned emerging from
project interventions. These knowledge products will be documented in lessons learned and best practice reports, as
well as in regular M&E reports, capturing successful approaches, lessons learned and strategies that have proven
effective in enhancing climate resilience at the community level. These reports will be made accessible to the public via
the NAP knowledge management platform and other channels appropriate to the local context (Activity 1.2.2).
By generating and sharing knowledge products that reflect community experiences and successful adaptation practices,
this activity will contribute to evidence-based decision-making and capacity building at local, national and regional
levels.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 1.2.1. are:
• Sub-activity 1.2.1.1. Hold bi-annual gender-inclusive discussions between representatives from the PMU, CTCs
and communities in the target hubs on project intervention successes and challenges, and develop these
discussions into community engagement reports.
• Sub-activity 1.2.1.2. Identify lessons learned and best practices used in project interventions, and develop these
into implementation guides and best practice reports.
Activity 1.2.2. Enhance the dissemination of adaptation knowledge to sub-national decision-makers and communities
to support upscaling.
To ensure that knowledge generated through the project is widely accessible and supports long-term climate change
adaptation efforts, the proposed project will facilitate the dissemination of knowledge products through both national
and international platforms. The primary mechanism for this will be the NAP knowledge management platform, which
is currently under development in Mauritania, where monitoring reports, GCF evaluation reports, community
engagement reports, records of the validation workshop, final policy briefs and best practices generated under Activity
1.2.1 will be uploaded and stored. This centralised repository will enhance access to critical adaptation information for
policymakers, practitioners and community-level stakeholders.

To extend the reach of these knowledge products beyond digital platforms, the project will strengthen the dissemination
of stored materials through community-based communication channels. A communications expert will be consulted to
design knowledge-management campaigns that are adapted to the local context. These campaigns will include targeted
knowledge-sharing efforts via local radio stations, TV programmes and printed materials such as flyers distributed by
DREDD officers. These efforts will ensure that information is accessible to local beneficiaries, including vulnerable
populations with limited internet access, thereby supporting enhances awareness, informed decision-making and the
replication of best practices at the community level.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 1.2.2. are:
• Sub-activity 1.2.2.1. Upload knowledge products (e.g. implementation guides, monitoring and evaluation reports,
community engagement reports, policy briefs, lessons learned and best practice reports) onto the NAP knowledge
management platform.
• Sub-activity 1.2.2.2. Package knowledge in the NAP knowledge management platform into formats that are
accessible at local level (e.g. brochures, TV and radio programmes, awareness-raising materials).
• Sub-activity 1.2.2.3. Disseminate locally-accessible knowledge products in target and non-target communes
across the four project wilayahs to catalyze upscaling, with support from the DREDDs and CTCs.
Outcome 2. Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania have increased
livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water access, and sustainable land- and
natural resources management practices.
Outcome 2 will enhance livelihood security for communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary
in Mauritania by promoting investment in Ecosystem-based Adaptation (EbA) measures and sustainable land and
natural resource-management practices.
The project will stabilise dunes and reduce sand encroachment by utilising EbA dune-fixation measures (green
infrastructure) within twelve priority communes across the Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema hubs. EbA dune
stabilisation techniques will include: i) establishing community-managed buffer zones, or protective greenbelts, along
priority communes’ borders; and ii) utilising both biological and mechanical dune-fixation techniques (described below)
to stabilise dunes in vulnerable areas identified by target communities. These EbA measures will be supplemented by
fencing (grey infrastructure), which will be installed around newly established dune-stabilisation sites to protect newly
planted vegetation from livestock.
Climate-resilient livestock-management practices will include rotational grazing, supplementary feeding and 'livestock
collar re-seeding'. By incorporating these climate-resilient livestock management practices, the project will enhance the
resilience of pastoral livelihoods and reduce pressure on natural resources. This approach will complement the
establishment of green-grey infrastructure to control sand encroachment and slow the rate of desertification.
Output 2.1. Green-grey dune fixation infrastructure is established to control sand encroachment, enhance the
provision of ecosystem services and slow the rate of desertification within the four target hubs.
Activity 2.1.1. Establish 2,123 hectares of EbA dune-fixation infrastructure and 120 kilometres of protective fencing
across the four target hubs, to facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance
ecosystem services related to dune stabilisation and the supply of natural resources.
Under this activity, ~2,123 hectares (ha) of EbA dune-fixation measures will be implemented across the four target
hubs. These measures will include:
• ~1,138 ha of protective green belts (buffer zones); and
• ~985 ha of mechanical and biological dune-fixation infrastructure.
Green belts in strategic locations will slow sand encroachment and subsequent desertification under future climate
change conditions, with natural vegetation serving as a barrier to approaching sand dunes (as shown in Figure 142 in
Annex 2). This will involve restoring degraded ecosystems and actively planting protective tree lines along the
boundaries of priority communes, to create buffer zones between vulnerable communities and mobile sand dunes (for
an example of protected tree lines, see Figure 143 in Annex 2). Each green belt will be ~500 m wide, with restored
ecosystems and planted treelines forming a continuous barrier against southward-shifting sand dunes. The strategic
positioning of these buffer zones along the north-eastern boundaries of Aoujeft, Rachid and Tamcheket will, in turn,
shield south-western communes against the impacts of sand encroachment in the long-term. Using GIS software, the
number of hectares of EbA dune-fixation measures needed to establish proposed buffer zones in each hub was
calculated (Table 2).

natural resources management practices.

Mechanical dune-fixation will involve the construction of windbreak hedges via wattling (Figure 145 in Annex 2Error!
Reference source not found.), to form ‘stop dunes’, or ‘check dunes’ (see Figure 146 in Annex 2). These structures
form a physical barrier between advancing sand dunes and target communities, while the checkerboard pattern assists
with capturing sand as it is carried by prevailing winds. Green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure will be installed along
the perimeter of each vulnerable town, to ensure that all critical infrastructure is loosely enclosed by treelines, wattling
and check dunes. Each of the five towns in Figure 147 in Annex 2 has an area of ~74 ha (740,000 m2); therefore, a
total of ~370 ha of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure (fences, wattling and check dunes) are needed to protect
critical infrastructure against sand encroachment at these sites.
Community members in the target hubs of Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema identified several important sites
requiring biological and mechanical dune-fixation during stakeholder consultations- outlined in Table 3 below. Details
regarding why these sites were selected are provided in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study. The total area of
mechanical- and biological dune fixation infrastructure required to protect vulnerable sites identified via GIS software
and stakeholder consultation is equivalent to 985 ha.

Fencing will be installed around green-grey dune-fixation sites, to protect vegetation against foraging and trampling by
free-roaming animals. Accordingly, fences will be erected around each of the dune-fixation sites identified in Table 3
above. The planting of treelines for biological dune fixation in buffer zones will follow the best practices described in
Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study and all tree seedlings used during planting exercises will be sourced from local
nurseries established under Activity 2.2.1, to support livelihood diversification efforts undertaken by the project.
Treelines will comprise a mixture of fast- and slow-growing, climate-resilient species, in accordance with the best
practices and lessons learned outlined in Section 6 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.
Regional livestock cooperatives and breeders’ associations exist within all of the proposed project’s target wilayah;
therefore, the proposed project will collaborate with representatives from these organisations — via the commune-level
CTCs — to facilitate the adoption of climate-resilient livestock-management practices. Measures will include replacing
local livestock breeds with climate-resilient breeds, growing climate-resilient fodder varieties for supplementary feeding
during the lean season25, and establishing ‘set-aside’ rotational grazing systems to rehabilitate degraded ecosystems
and rangelands. Additionally, pastoral water points and livestock corridors will be established to reverse sedentarisation
trends among historically transhumant pastoralist communities, reducing anthropogenic pressure on natural
ecosystems and minimising conflict between land users competing for natural resources. By employing these strategies,
livestock farmers’ resilience to climate change-induced desertification within the four target hubs will be enhanced.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.1.1. are:

• Sub-activity 2.1.1.1. Support CTCs to co-develop commune-level land rehabilitation plans in collaboration with
village-level stakeholders, members of project management teams and DREDD representatives.
• Sub-activity 2.1.1.2. Install ~2,123 ha of dune-stabilisation infrastructure (1,138 ha of green belts; 985 ha of

critical areas against the impacts of sand inundation.

to protect biological dune-fixation infrastructure against damage from livestock and unsustainable use of natural

management practices, such as rotational grazing, transhumance, supplementary feeding, agro-silvopasture,
collective herding and 'livestock collar re-seeding'.
Output 2.2. Improved access to water for land rehabilitation and agricultural activities.
Activity 2.2.1. Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) and commune-level water monitoring and
regulation plans.
Commune-level water user groups (WUGs) will be formed in the 12 priority communes. Each WUG will be led by elected
community leaders from the respective priority communes and will report to the CTCs. WUG members will comprise
local community members and representatives from livestock cooperatives, farmer-based organisations and women's
groups in each target commune. WUGs will be trained to monitor water availability and usage within their respective
communes and to oversee and manage the construction, operation and maintenance of infrastructure for capturing
surface water, managing runoff flow, increasing groundwater recharge and reducing flood risks.
A team of national experts — comprising two hydrogeologists from the Ministry of Water and Sanitation (MHA26) and
two meteorologists from the Meteorological Service of Mauritania (ONM) — will conduct hydrogeological studies to
quantify and map groundwater and surface water resources within each of the target hubs. In particular, this team will
be mandated to identify flood-risk areas and determine the interconnectivity of aquifers, informing the selection of
strategic sites to install: i) groundwater dams, for improving aquifer recharge; ii) gabions, dikes and stone bunds for
reducing flood risks; and iii) reservoirs, boreholes, well points and rainwater-harvesting systems to increase rainwater
capture and storage. The selection of target sites for water management infrastructure will be summarised in the form
of commune-level water management plans.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.2.1. are:
• Sub-activity 2.2.1.1. Establish community-managed Water User Groups (WUGs) within each target commune and
train members to implement and maintain water-related activities introduced under Activity 2.2.2 of the project.
• Sub-activity 2.2.1.2. Support CTCs to raise awareness about sustainable water usage and co-develop commune-
level water monitoring and regulation plans in collaboration with WUGs.
• Sub-activity 2.2.1.3. Conduct hydrogeological studies (or consult existing hydrogeological maps, where applicable)
and engage with WUG members to identify priority sites to install water-management infrastructure (Sub-activities
2.2.2.1, 2.2.2.2 and 2.2.3.1) in each target commune. Summarise the finding of site selection into a commune-level
water management plan for each priority commune.
Activity 2.2.2. Install physical water management infrastructure — including weirs, gabions, dikes, stone- and earthen
bunds, groundwater dams and water access points for pastoralists.
Under Activity 2.2.2, water security within the proposed project’s target hubs will be strengthened by installing flood
management infrastructure — including weirs, gabions, dikes, stone bunds, earthen bunds and groundwater dams —
in each priority commune. These measures will reduce surface water runoff, increase groundwater infiltration and
recharge rates, and enhance the capture of rainfall for use in supporting sustainable agriculture and climate-resilient
livelihoods.
Additionally, pastoral water access points — that is, boreholes equipped with solar pumps — will be established in
graras and along historical transhumance routes, to support year-round agricultural production and transhumance and
reverse sedentarisation trends among nomadic pastoralists. These physical measures will: i) improve the availability of
fresh water for use in agriculture and livestock farming; ii) reduce unsustainable abstraction from aquifers; and iii) ensure
the long-term sustainability of climate-resilient livestock management, agricultural practices and livelihoods introduced
under Output 2.3.

The extent of physical water-management infrastructure installed within each target hub should not exceed: i) four
groundwater dams (each with a storage capacity of ~10,000 m3); ii) 4 km of clay dikes; iii) 4 km of stone gabions; and
iv) 80 ha of stone bunds arranged in 50 x 50 m squares. Details regarding site selection for these interventions are
provided in Section 6 and 7 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.2.2. are:
• Sub-activity 2.2.2.1. Install physical water management infrastructure — including weirs, gabions, dikes, stone-
and earthen bunds, groundwater dams and solar-powered pumps — at strategic sites within each target commune,
to improve water access and availability, increase groundwater recharge rates and reduce flood risks in the target
hubs.
• Sub-activity 2.2.2.2. Establish water access points along historical transhumance routes and in graras, to improve
nomadic pastoralists' access to water and reduce sedentarisation among livestock herders.
Activity 2.2.3. Install 12 rainwater-harvesting systems and communal cisterns (5,000 L per system) within each target
commune
Under Activity 2.2.3 of the proposed project, the installation of rainwater harvesting (RWH) systems and communal
cisterns will increase the capture of surface water runoff during extreme rainfall events and increase target communities’
capacities to store water for use during prolonged periods of drought. To promote fair access, sites located near
communal buildings (town halls, schools and health posts) and agricultural areas (including market gardens and
nurseries) will be given preference, to ensure water is used for agricultural activities and sustainable livelihoods
introduced under the proposed project.
To establish the RWH systems and communal cisterns, local labour will be employed and supervised by trained WUG
members. Twelve RWH systems and communal cisterns, with a capacity of 5,000 litres each, will be distributed to each
of the 12 priority communes, totalling 144 RWH systems and cisterns across the four target hubs. Details regarding site
selection for these interventions are provided in Section 6 and 7 of Annex 2: Feasibility Study.
Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.2.2. are:
• Sub-activity 2.2.3.1. Install 12 rainwater-harvesting systems and communal cisterns (5000L per system) within
each target commune across the four hubs, to improve access to water for agricultural livelihood activities.
Output 2.3. Climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods based on sustainable land- and natural resource-use are developed
and/or strengthened to reduce land degradation and support climate-resilient income-generation by community
members within the target regions.
Activity 2.3.1. Facilitate the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices and sustainable, diversified livelihoods by
farmers within the target communes
The project will facilitate the adoption of climate-resilient practices (Activity 2.3.1). The project will encourage livelihood
diversification by supporting the uptake of sustainable income-generating activities such as beekeeping, poultry farming
and value-added processing, reducing dependence on traditional agricultural activities and building resilience against
climate-related risks. To further improve rural farmers’ resilience to the impacts of climate-change desertification, MEDD
and the Ministry of Rural Development (MDR) extension officers will supply local nurseries with an initial stock of climate-
resilient27 seed varietals for sale and distribution to local farmers and market gardeners within each of the target hubs
and train nursery operators to maintain sustainable stocks (or seed banks).
Horticultural centres established in each priority commune will be supplied with the initial materials needed to
commence business operations. These may include beehives for apiculture, climate-resilient seed varietals for seed
banks and irrigation kits for market gardens. Access to markets to sell horticultural produce will be improved by: i)
organising weekly markets in each priority commune — arranged by CTCs — to facilitate trade between local
community members; and ii) using ‘community carts’28 to transport goods to larger, urban markets within the target hubs
and surrounding areas. Please refer to Table 82 in Annex 2 for a summary of the types of climate-smart agriculture
(CSA) practices currently implemented and Table 84 in Annex 2 for a summary of potential alternative livelihoods.
27 Crop varietals with drought- and heat-resistant genotypes

to be planted in protected areas, water for agriculture and nurseries, and stones for the construction of stone bunds. This transport can be
used by individuals for a small fee, to generate income and maintain the condition of animals

Sub-activities contributing to the delivery of Activity 2.3.1 are:
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.1. Establish nurseries and seed banks in each target commune to supply activities related to
land rehabilitation and dune fixation (Activity 2.1.1), CSA practices (Sub-activity 2.3.1.3) and horticultural activities
such as market-gardening (Sub-activity 2.3.1.4).
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.2. Collect cuttings and seeds from agricultural crop species, as well as indigenous grass and
tree species, to serve as stock material for nurseries and seed banks established under Sub-activity 2.3.1.1.
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.3. Train farmers within the target communes to practice climate-resilient crop agriculture and
use improved agricultural technologies, including drip irrigation kits, solar powered pumps, integrated pest
management strategies, zai pits and half-moons.
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.4. Conduct site visits and provide technical support to facilitate the uptake of sustainable
livelihood activities — including horticulture (market-gardening), apiculture, poultry farming, livestock feed
production and the collection and sale of non-timber forest products — by community members within the target
communes.
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.5. Supply farmers and horticulturalists with water-efficient irrigation equipment and climate-
resilient crop varieties to support the uptake of agricultural activities adopted under Sub-activities 2.3.1.3 and
2.3.1.4.
• Sub-activity 2.3.1.6. Improve access to urban markets and develop value chains for offloading agricultural produce
within each target commune, to enhance income generated from sustainable agricultural livelihoods.
Activity 2.3.2. Establish a small grants facility to facilitate continued investment in upscaling successful EbA activities
and sustainable livelihoods.
A project Small Grants Facility (SGF) will be established to enhance local communities’ access to finance to upscale
successful EbA interventions and sustainable, agriculture-based livelihoods introduced under the project. The on-
granting mechanism will promote and support community-led initiatives, fostering the autonomous uptake of EbA
practices by community members. GCF Proceeds will be used to finance the sub-grants. The SGF will be housed in
MEDD, and sub-grant agreements will be executed between MEDD and each sub-grant recipient.
An SGF Steering Committee will be instituted to guide and oversee the establishment and operations of the SGF. It will
be chaired by UNEP as the project AE, and will include representation from MEDD and other relevant Ministries (the
Ministry of Economy and Finance; the Ministry of Agriculture; the Ministry of Livestock Farming; the Ministry of Housing,
Urbanism and Regional Planning), as well as from UNDP (to ensure links and synergies with the Small Grants
Programme it manages). The SGF Steering Committee will review the recommended funding proposals and jointly
make the grant award decisions. UNEP as the chair of the SGF Steering Committee will report on its operations to the
PSC. Table 81 in Annex 2 outlines the preliminary proposed design of the SGF, and the process for submitting and
screening sub-grant proposals. The screening begins with CTCs (including representatives of vulnerable groups and
CSOs) and is completed by the SGF team housed in MEDD (see below), before submission to the SGF Steering
Committee.
Establishing and operating a project SGF requires procuring an SGF management team to be housed in MEDD, to: i)
oversee the facility’s operation and maintenance; and ii) manage the approval, disbursement and monitoring of small
grants throughout the project implementation period. Prior to the SGF establishment, an international consultant
(finance expert) will develop the SGF’s policies, frameworks and guiding principles. These will be built on lessons
learned and best practice from other projects in the region. For example, the experience with the project Enhancing
resilience of communities to the adverse effects of climate change on food security in Mauritania (PARSACC) showed
that building social and capital was a key success factor. The international consultant will also design a funding proposal
template and accompanying selection criteria for the SGF, streamlining the small-grants approval process.
Capacity building support will be provided to MEDD to strengthen MEDD’s institutional, operational and financial
capacity for the design, execution and oversight of the SGF. This consultancy will also provide technical backstopping
to MEDD on these aspects throughout the implementation of the SGF. As a first step, building on the findings of the
HACT micro-assessment undertaken in 2023, specific capacity or process gaps will be identified that may impact the
management of the SGF. In addition, experiences and lessons learnt from MEDD’s ongoing small grants programme
will be reviewed. This programme has been operational since 2022, with $250,000 awarded annually to approximately
20 local NGOs, cooperatives and other CSOs for climate change adaptation and environmental initiatives.
Furthermore, to increase the capacity of potential recipients of the grants, a suitable service provider will be contracted
to work with potential recipients to: i) strengthen their organisational and financial management capacity; ii) increase
their capacity to develop and submit project concepts so as to access funds from the SGF; and iii) improve their capacity
to successfully implement EbA measures funded through the SGF. It is anticipated that 5 projects per year in each of

Accredited Entity
UNEP is the Accredited Entity (AE) for this project, as designated by the Government of the Islamic Republic
of Mauritania (GoM). The AE will be responsible for the overall management of the project as well as for the
financial monitoring and reporting aspects of the proposed project’s activities. UNEP will be responsible for
overseeing the implementation, monitoring and evaluation (M&E) of the project in coordination with the Project
Steering Committee (PSC) and the Project Management Unit (PMU). A UNEP Programme Officer (PO) will be
responsible for project supervision to ensure compliance with GCF’s and UNEP’s standard policies and
operating procedures. The PO will be required to participate in annual Project Steering Committee (PSC)
meetings. The PO will be supported by a UNEP Fund Management Officer (FMO) on financial monitoring and
supervision.
Experience and track record of AE
UNEP has a broad global portfolio on climate change projects funded via the, inter alia, GCF, Least Developed
Countries Fund (LDCF), Special Climate Change Fund (SCCF), Adaptation Fund (AF) and bilateral funds such
as the European Commission. From these projects, UNEP has supported national governments and local
communities to adapt to climate change. UNEP’s knowledge-base is derived from completed and ongoing
projects supporting: i) methods and tools for decision-making; ii) prioritising, designing and implementing
adaptation interventions; iii) enhancing climate resilience by restoring vulnerable ecosystems that underpin
community livelihoods; and iv) monitoring the socioeconomic and environmental benefits of adaptation
interventions.
UNEP also has a longstanding relationship in Mauritania, having supported the country in GEF enabling
activities, including the development of the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) as well as
national communications since 1997. UNEP is also currently supporting the GCF National Adaptation Plan
(NAP) Readiness Programme and has assisted the implementation of several CCA projects in Mauritania with

=== PAGE 23 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 19

the support of international climate change funds, including the GEF-funded projects, EbA South and DIMS.
As the GCF AE, UNEP will draw on previous experience and lessons learned to oversee the efficient and
effective delivery of the project objectives during the implementation of the proposed project.
Executing Entity
The proposed project will be implemented over a six-year period from 2025 to 2030 and executed by
Mauritania’s Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD). The MEDD is tasked with
developing and executing the National Sustainable Development Plan (SNEDD), implementing the National
Environmental Action Plan (PANE II), formulating operational plans, and overseeing and securing financial and
material support for program implementation. It has created sector-specific plans and programmes concerning
desertification and climate change, biodiversity, wetland conservation, and coastal ordinance. These areas of
expertise and context-specific knowledge allow the MEDD to provide the necessary oversight and guidance
during the implementation of the project. The MEDD also serves as the country's focal point for the United
Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). Moreover, the Coordination Cell for the
National Climate Change Programme (Cellule de Coordination du Programme National sur le Changement
Climatique, CCPNCC) operates within the MEDD to coordinate and implement climate change initiatives in the
country, as well as handle reporting, such as the National Communications III. Both the MEDD and CCPNCC
consult directly with several ministries and departments to promote these programs.
As the Executing Entity (EE), the MEDD will coordinate the implementation of the project at national level and
will report to UNEP as the AE. To ensure that the project remains in line with national policies, MEDD and
UNEP will establish a Project Steering Committee (discussed below).
Project Steering Committee
To ensure project compliance with national policies, MEDD and UNEP will establish a Project Steering
Committee (PSC). This committee will be composed of at least one representative from the MEDD, the Ministry
of Agriculture; the Ministry of Livestock Farming; the Ministry of Water and Sanitation; and the Ministry of
Housing, Urbanism and Regional Planning, respectively. In addition, to ensure local representation, a
representative of regional councils (rotating between project regions), a mayor representing communes
(rotating between communes) and a CSO representative will be included in the PSC. A representative of UNEP
will also form part of the PSC. This committee will have a decision-making capacity and will primarily serve to
provide project oversight and advisory support, including: a) overseeing project implementation; and b)
reviewing annual budget and workplans. The committee will meet at least twice a year (once per semester),
with ad hoc meetings as required to discuss project results and performance indicators and provide strategic
guidance.
Technical Committee
In addition to the PSC, a Technical Committee will be established under the chairmanship of the Ministry of
Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) to provide technical guidance and ensure ownership,
communication and reporting on the project with national adaptation communities. This Technical Committee
will be composed of one representative from the Ministry of Economic Affairs, Development and Finance, the
Ministry of Interior and Decentralization, the Ministry of Transportation and Infrastructure, the Ministry of
Petroleum, Energy and Mines, the Ministry of Social Affairs, Children and Family, the Ministry of Higher
Education and Scientific Research, and the National Bureau of Statistics, respectively. The Technical
Committee will also request specific technical support from other relevant stakeholders, as required. These
stakeholders will include tertiary education institutions in Mauritania, notably the Nouakchott University El
Asriya (UNEA), Ecole Normale Supérieure (ENS), Scientific Institute for Technology Education (ISET) or the
Mauritanian Institute for Scientific Research (IMRS). The Technical Committee will seek cooperation
opportunities with potential project partners including, inter alia, UNDP Country Office, UNOPS, IFAD, FAO,
World Bank, AfDB, AFD, GIZ, CIDA and IUCN, as well as national NGOs and private sector associations. The
Technical Committee will report to the PSC and will meet at least twice a year (once per semester), with ad
hoc meetings held when required.
Project Management Unit
The PMU will be embedded within the MEDD and will consist of: i) a Project Coordinator (PC); ii) a Financial
and Administrative Officer; iii) a Procurement Officer; iv) a Chief Technical Advisor (CTA); v) a Communications
Officer; vi) a Gender Officer; vii) an Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Officer; and viii) a Monitoring
and Evaluation Officer.
