The PC will be a full-time, competitively recruited staff member, responsible for the daily implementation and
management of project activities. The PC will: i) report directly to the PSC on project management-related
matters; ii) manage the project in accordance with the specified workplans and allocated budget; iii) ensure
that all project interventions are implemented according to GCF and UNEP guidelines; iv) manage and disburse
financial resources allocated for the project during project implementation; v) work closely with national and
local authorities to ensure that the project is managed effectively and that the needs of all beneficiary groups
are considered; and vi) oversee the efficient and effective information and knowledge-transfer to relevant
project partners. All PMU members will report to the PC. The CTA will provide technical guidance at all stages
of project implementation, to ensure project interventions are implemented effectively and efficiently, in line
with the latest best practices and lessons learned from around the globe. The CTA will be a UNEP National
Officer. A part-time Communications Officer will be responsible for managing all awareness-raising and
knowledge disseminations activities over the course of the proposed project.
Implementing Partners
A local NGO in Mauritania, Association Naforé 29, has been identified as an eligible implementing partner for
the proposed project. Naforé will assist the MEDD to implement on-the-ground interventions (Outputs 2.1, 2.2
and 2.3) within the proposed project’s four target hubs. Nafore will report directly to the MEDD and collaborate
with the PMU to implement ecosystem restoration, livelihood and water infrastructure activities. Additional
Implementation Partners may be onboarded during the project implementation stage.
Since its inception in 2009, Naforé has implemented several projects in the domains of environmental
protection, health, hygiene and sanitation, showcasing its capacity through diverse projects and a wide range
of expertise among its members. Naforé’s main strengths are summarised below:
• Organisational strength: Naforé boasts a membership base of 25 individuals from various professional
backgrounds, contributing to its rich pool of expertise and allowing it to respond adeptly to environmental,
health, hygiene and sanitation challenges.
• Mission and objectives: The organisation is dedicated to supporting state policies focused on
environmental protection and poverty alleviation using participatory approaches that involve all
stakeholders. Its goals include contributing to state environmental protection policies, improving the living
conditions of disadvantaged groups through income-generating activities that are compatible with natural
resource conservation, and participating in national, sub-regional or international actions promoting
childhood support and indigent care.
• Environmental initiatives: Naforé is actively engaged in conservation efforts that address challenges
related to biodiversity, species protection, habitat protection, natural resource conservation, climate
change mitigation, waste management, pollution control, sustainable agriculture and rural hygiene and
sanitation. These initiatives reflect a comprehensive approach to environmental stewardship, with the aim
of maintaining biodiversity, supporting sustainable land and natural resource use and enhancing
community resilience to climate change and its effects.
• Project execution: Naforé has a proven track record of successfully managing projects funded by several
national and international bodies, including inter alia UNDP, IUCN, UNICEF, FAO and GIZ. Projects such
as promoting green jobs for rural youth, environmental awareness campaigns and improving hygiene and
sanitation in healthcare centres demonstrate Naforé's ability to effectively utilise funds and achieve project
goals.
• Strategic partnerships: The organisation has built strong partnerships with state agencies, international
institutions and local communities, showcasing its collaborative approach and ability to work within the
broader framework of Mauritania’s development policies.
• Expertise in environmental impact studies: Naforé has been involved in significant environmental
impact studies for several sectors, including oil exploration and infrastructure development. These activities
highlight its expertise in assessing and mitigating environmental impacts in line with national and
international standards.
Operational arrangements
• Key contractual agreements: The partnership between GCF, UNEP, the MEDD and other Implementing
Partners is governed by a set of legal agreements outlining the scope, responsibilities, and financial
arrangements of the project. These agreements define the operational framework and set the foundation
for project execution.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
Grant finance from the GCF is the most appropriate form of concessional grant finance for the proposed project.
This is because there are limited financial resources for investment in climate-resilient sustainable development
in Mauritania, which is a considerable barrier to the implementation of CCA interventions in the country. In
addition, there is a high level of public and external debt in Mauritania, which limits the capacity of the GoM to
repay loans. Moreover, the country is a least developed country (LDC), with low income and severe structural
impediments to sustainable development. As a result, communities in Mauritania require support from the GCF
to achieve a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development.
Communities living along the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania are highly vulnerable to the impacts of
climate change, particularly the increasing rates of desertification, dune advancement and water scarcity. In
2019, Mauritania was ranked the 20th most vulnerable country to climate change and other global challenges

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)
Grant finance from the GCF is the most appropriate form of concessional grant finance for the proposed project.
This is because there are limited financial resources for investment in climate-resilient sustainable development
in Mauritania, which is a considerable barrier to the implementation of CCA interventions in the country. In
addition, there is a high level of public and external debt in Mauritania, which limits the capacity of the GoM to
repay loans. Moreover, the country is a least developed country (LDC), with low income and severe structural
impediments to sustainable development. As a result, communities in Mauritania require support from the GCF
to achieve a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development.
Communities living along the Sahara-Sahel boundary of Mauritania are highly vulnerable to the impacts of
climate change, particularly the increasing rates of desertification, dune advancement and water scarcity. In
2019, Mauritania was ranked the 20th most vulnerable country to climate change and other global challenges

=== PAGE 26 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 22
B
on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) country index. Notably, the country had the highest
overall sensitivity score30, largely because of prevailing insecurity within the food and water sectors31.
Moreover, of the 182 countries on the ND-GAIN readiness index, Mauritania was ranked the 75th least ready
to respond to climate change and other global challenges, with education and control of corruption comprising
the worst-scoring readiness indicators in the country32. As a result, there is an urgent need for support to
improve the adaptive capacity of rural communities in Mauritania. Despite this, the GoM has limited resources
to implement CCA, as is further specified in the constraints summarised below.
Development challenges in targeted rural communities
The vulnerability of Mauritania’s population is high because of elevated levels of poverty and a strong
dependence on ‘climate change sensitive’ sectors, including agriculture, fisheries, mining and livestock33. In
addition, access to basic services and facilities is unequal between rural and urban areas and major obstacles
are encountered by those attempting to transition out of poverty34. As a result, approximately 6% of Mauritania’s
population live below the international poverty line (2011 purchasing power parity US$1.9 per day)35 and ~28%
below the national poverty line36. Moreover, 70% of the country’s impoverished households live in rural areas
and are mainly engaged in agriculture and livestock-rearing. This high prevalence of poverty and limited access
to basic resources means that rural communities living within the four target hubs lack the adaptive and
financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient sustainable development and are almost entirely dependent on
external support from both government and development partners.
Public debt and fiscal constraints at the national level
Mauritania's Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) to the UNFCCC states that major
investments totalling USD9.4 billion are required for the Mauritanian population to adequately adapt between
2015 and 2030 to the impacts of climate change. It is further stated that USD8.2 billion is required to reduce
projected emissions in the country by 22% before 2030. However, the high level of public debt (99% of GDP
in 2016) constrains public financing for CCA, making it difficult for the GoM to secure financing for adaptation
projects. Moreover, the fiscal capacity of the GoM to meet the commitments of the NDC without external
support is further reduced by recent volatility in global financial markets as a result of the Russia-Ukraine War.
As a result, baseline development issues are often prioritised in the constrained budget of the country, resulting
in limited climate change adaptation and mitigation action.
The GoM is actively working to mobilise funding towards the INDC and large investments have already been
made in climate change adaptation. For example, the GoM spent USD335 million between 2011 and 2015 to
provide support to the drought-affected population, and invested USD330 million in poverty alleviation between
2006 and 2010 — particularly focusing on land management in agropastoral areas. However, further
investments to address climate change are almost entirely reliant on assistance from external multilateral
development partners, including the GCF. Consequently, the proposed outcomes, outputs and activities of this
project have been designed to be fully aligned with the objectives of the GCF, with technical support provided
by UNEP (see B.4. section).
Justification for level of concessionality
The proposed interventions aim to improve subsistence-level agriculture, water access, and health outcomes
in marginalized communities. Grant funding is proposed as much of the value generated accrues to households
through local consumption instead of through formal value chains and trade, which would generate the
sufficient cashflows needed to sustain alternative funding instruments. In addition, the i) high levels of
interdimensional poverty in Mauritania; ii) the high debt-to-GDP ratio of the GoM; and iii) the low levels of
economic development in the country adds to the financial-level risk in the project. Furthermore, there is limited
incentive for the private sector to participate in the proposed project because: i) there is limited presence of
private sector entities in the target areas; and ii) the project has a low potential for cost-recovery. As a result,
the private sector is excluded as an option for alternative funding for the proposed project.
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
30 Reflects the extent to which the country depends on ‘climate change sensitive’ sectors.
31 WB Group. 2021. Climate change knowledge portal: Mauritania — vulnerability.
32 University of Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative. 2022. Country index — Mauritania.
33 WB Group. 2021. Climate change knowledge portal: Mauritania — vulnerability.
34 WB Group. 2020. Poverty and equity brief — Mauritania.
35 WB Group. 2020. Poverty and equity brief — Mauritania.
36 United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees (UNHCR) Mauritania. 2021. Protection and solutions strategy.

--- Table 1 on Page 26 ---
on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) country index. Notably, the country had the highest
overall sensitivity score30, largely because of prevailing insecurity within the food and water sectors31.
Moreover, of the 182 countries on the ND-GAIN readiness index, Mauritania was ranked the 75th least ready
to respond to climate change and other global challenges, with education and control of corruption comprising
the worst-scoring readiness indicators in the country32. As a result, there is an urgent need for support to
improve the adaptive capacity of rural communities in Mauritania. Despite this, the GoM has limited resources
to implement CCA, as is further specified in the constraints summarised below.
Development challenges in targeted rural communities
The vulnerability of Mauritania’s population is high because of elevated levels of poverty and a strong
dependence on ‘climate change sensitive’ sectors, including agriculture, fisheries, mining and livestock33. In
addition, access to basic services and facilities is unequal between rural and urban areas and major obstacles
are encountered by those attempting to transition out of poverty34. As a result, approximately 6% of Mauritania’s
population live below the international poverty line (2011 purchasing power parity US$1.9 per day)35 and ~28%
below the national poverty line36. Moreover, 70% of the country’s impoverished households live in rural areas
and are mainly engaged in agriculture and livestock-rearing. This high prevalence of poverty and limited access
to basic resources means that rural communities living within the four target hubs lack the adaptive and
financial capacity to invest in climate-resilient sustainable development and are almost entirely dependent on
external support from both government and development partners.
Public debt and fiscal constraints at the national level
Mauritania's Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) to the UNFCCC states that major
investments totalling USD9.4 billion are required for the Mauritanian population to adequately adapt between
2015 and 2030 to the impacts of climate change. It is further stated that USD8.2 billion is required to reduce
projected emissions in the country by 22% before 2030. However, the high level of public debt (99% of GDP
in 2016) constrains public financing for CCA, making it difficult for the GoM to secure financing for adaptation
projects. Moreover, the fiscal capacity of the GoM to meet the commitments of the NDC without external
support is further reduced by recent volatility in global financial markets as a result of the Russia-Ukraine War.
As a result, baseline development issues are often prioritised in the constrained budget of the country, resulting
in limited climate change adaptation and mitigation action.
The GoM is actively working to mobilise funding towards the INDC and large investments have already been
made in climate change adaptation. For example, the GoM spent USD335 million between 2011 and 2015 to
provide support to the drought-affected population, and invested USD330 million in poverty alleviation between
2006 and 2010 — particularly focusing on land management in agropastoral areas. However, further
investments to address climate change are almost entirely reliant on assistance from external multilateral
development partners, including the GCF. Consequently, the proposed outcomes, outputs and activities of this
project have been designed to be fully aligned with the objectives of the GCF, with technical support provided
by UNEP (see B.4. section).
Justification for level of concessionality
The proposed interventions aim to improve subsistence-level agriculture, water access, and health outcomes
in marginalized communities. Grant funding is proposed as much of the value generated accrues to households
through local consumption instead of through formal value chains and trade, which would generate the
sufficient cashflows needed to sustain alternative funding instruments. In addition, the i) high levels of
interdimensional poverty in Mauritania; ii) the high debt-to-GDP ratio of the GoM; and iii) the low levels of
economic development in the country adds to the financial-level risk in the project. Furthermore, there is limited
incentive for the private sector to participate in the proposed project because: i) there is limited presence of
private sector entities in the target areas; and ii) the project has a low potential for cost-recovery. As a result,
the private sector is excluded as an option for alternative funding for the proposed project.
B.6. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

=== PAGE 27 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23
B
Sustainability through technical and institutional capacity building
The project will create a strengthened enabling environment that will support the long-term sustainability and
scaling of CCA measures in Mauritania. This enabling environment will include: i) improved vertical (across
national, regional and local levels) and horizontal (between government departments at the hub level)
coordination mechanisms, including the commune-level technical committees (CTCs); ii) updated policies,
plans and strategies that mainstream CCA and natural resource management in a gender-responsive manner;
and iii) strengthened knowledge management systems aimed at collecting, tailoring and disseminating CCA
best practices. This will empower relevant government institutions at the national and sub-national levels to
dedicate more resources and budget to incorporating gender-responsive CCA initiatives into development
plans, policies and budgets.
Equipment provided through the project will also provide recipients (GoM, CTCs, cooperatives, associations
etc.) with the technical capacity to continue implementing CCA measures. To ensure that this remains
functional, the GoM will assume responsibility for the continued operation and maintenance of this equipment
after the project lifespan. A structured plan, with responsible entities, estimated costs and funding sources will
be developed as part of the project’s exit strategy. In cases where the equipment is provided to community
user groups (e.g. water user groups or natural resource management associations), a management system
will be established, with a fee structure that generates funds for the operation, maintenance and repair of the
equipment. Where needed, maintenance costs will be integrated into government (MEDD) budgets, with a set
schedule for inspections to identify maintenance needs. This will promote the sustainability of the CCA
measures.
Social and environmental sustainability
The geographical isolation of the target hubs means that community participation and ownership of the
proposed project’s on-the-ground interventions are critical to their longevity during and past the project’s
lifespan. Government stakeholders have highlighted this participatory approach as a critical component to
ensure the success and longevity of project interventions. Community and decision-makers participation will
be secured through the CTCs, WUGs and other community level groups.
Financial sustainability
Several financing opportunities at the government and community levels will enable the continued O&M of
interventions beyond the project’s lifespan. At the government level, the prioritisation of CCA, gender
considerations and natural resource management into policies, plans and strategies will promote the allocation
of sufficient funds to CCA initiatives through national, regional and local government budgets.
A critical component of achieving financial sustainability will be establishment of an on-granting mechanism
under Activity 2.3.2, designed to provide financial stability and facilitate continued investment in upscaling
successful EbA activities and sustainable livelihoods. To ensure the longevity and effectiveness of this
mechanism beyond the project lifespan, the on-granting mechanism will be housed in MEDD, which has the
mandate and capacity to manage and scale CCA and EbA initiatives. This entity will be responsible for
overseeing the on-granting mechanism, ensuring that it continues to support community-led initiatives and
foster the autonomous adoption and upscaling of successful EbA measures and sustainable agricultural-based
livelihoods within the target hubs.
To solidify this transition, the project will work closely with the designated government entity to build its capacity
in fund management, monitoring and evaluation, as well as in the strategic allocation of resources towards
high-impact EbA interventions. This collaboration will include the development of clear guidelines for fund
disbursement, criteria for project selection, and mechanisms for tracking and reporting on the impact of funded
projects. The on-granting mechanism will be embedded within an existing governmental framework to
encourage directing a percentage of regional government development funds into the SGF. The long-term
viability of this mechanism will be supported by the development of a monitoring and reporting system that will
ensure the traceability and risk management of funds between the SGF and local-level stakeholders.
At the household level, the financial benefits incurred through improved: i) climate-resilient agricultural and
alternative livelihoods; ii) access to water, food and wood fuel; iii) protection from permanent losses; and iv)
built and natural capital through dune stabilisation are expected to incentivise individuals to maintain introduced
practices, equipment and infrastructure after the project has concluded. In total, the project’s economic benefit
to cost ratio is estimated at 5.86 to 1 (Annex 3: FEA and Annex 3b). Additionally, technical support will be given
to communities to facilitate market access for alternative livelihoods introduced by the project, which will assist
in ensuring the long-term viability of these livelihoods.

=== PAGE 28 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 24
B
It is assumed that the capacity built through the project within institutions and among individuals will be retained
post-project, ensuring the continuity of skills and knowledge necessary for the ongoing application and scaling
of CCA measures. This retention will be facilitated by: i) institutionalising training programmes; ii) embedding
CCA and sustainable natural resource management practices into standard operating procedures; and iii)
fostering a culture of continuous learning and adaptation within participating organisations.

C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

C.3.2. Does GCF funding finance technology

development/transfer?
Technology transfer
Outcome 1 of the proposed project will create a conducive environment for climate change and gender-responsive land-
use planning and natural resource management in Mauritania, facilitating technology transfer through several key
strategies. First, by strengthening the institutional capacity of decision-making bodies, Outcome 1 will enable the
adoption and implementation of new technologies by national- and sub-national decision-makers. Knowledge sharing
and dissemination are promoted under Outcome 1, by training stakeholders to leverage the NAP Knowledge
Management Platform to facilitate the exchange of information and best practices. Overall, this outcome promotes a
supportive environment that encourages capacity building, knowledge sharing, financial support, collaboration and long-
term sustainability across all four target hubs, enabling beneficiaries to adopt and scale up innovative technologies to
address climate change challenges and improve natural resource management in Mauritania.
Outcome 2 of the proposed project will facilitate technology transfer by implementing EbA interventions to restore the
provisioning of ecosystems services towards water infiltration and control of sand encroachment in the four target hubs.
These interventions will include establishing ~2,123 ha of green-grey dune-stabilisation infrastructure — using
ecosystem restoration and biological- and mechanical dune fixation measures — at strategic sites within twelve priority
communes across the four rural hubs. The installation of this infrastructure will contribute to ~65,700 individuals in the
target hubs experiencing improved protection from the increasing impacts of sand inundation. Approximately 120km of
fencing will be established around dune-fixation sites, to ensure the protection of these sites and the ecosystem services
accrued within them in the long-term.
The resilience of target communities to the impacts of drought will also be strengthened via the installation of physical
structures such as gabions, dikes, boreholes, solar pumps, groundwater dams for improved aquifer recharge and
rainwater harvesting systems. These structures will enhance the water security of more than 85,000 individuals across
the target hubs by increasing groundwater storage and reducing overland water flow during heavy rainfall events,
thereby limiting the amount of water lost through evaporation.
Capacity-building interventions
The engagement of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) as the Executing Entity for the
project will contribute significantly to building the government’s technical, operational and project management capacity.
In addition to supporting MEDD in addressing any procurement, human resource and/or financial management capacity
gaps identified in the HACT micro-assessment and procurement assessment, the project interventions include specific
capacity building support to strengthen MEDD’s institutional, operational and financial capacity for the design, execution
and oversight of the Small Grants Facility.
Capacity-building activities under Outcome 1 include strengthening the legal and institutional framework of Mauritania
for sustainable natural resource management and strategic land-use planning, to build the resilience of the rural
Mauritanian population against the impacts of climate change-induced drought and desertification. Overall, the project
will create a strengthened enabling environment that will support the long-term sustainability and scaling of climate
change adaptation measures in Mauritania. This enabling environment will include: i) improved vertical and horizontal
coordination mechanisms; ii) updated policies, plans and strategies that mainstream climate change adaptation and
natural resource management; and iii) strengthened knowledge management systems aimed at collecting, tailoring and
disseminating best practices. This will empower relevant government institutions at the national and sub-national levels
to dedicate more resources and budget to incorporating gender-responsive climate change adaptation initiatives into
development plans, policies and budgets.
Capacity-building initiatives implemented under Outcome 2 largely involve training community members on how to
design, implement, manage EbA dune-stabilisation infrastructure, water resources management infrastructure and CSA
interventions. The establishment and training of Water User Groups, for example, will strengthen the communities’
capacity to monitor water availability and usage, and to oversee and manage the construction, operation and
maintenance of water infrastructure. Additionally, capacity-building under Outcome 2 includes training local community

members to uptake sustainable, alternative livelihoods in the four target hubs. The total cost of all capacity-building
initiatives to be implemented under the project is USD7,200,000. This estimate includes all costs associated with
Outcome 1 and all training activities and workshops implemented under Outcome 2.
Under the proposed project, ~1,800 national and sub-national decision-makers will be trained on how to design,
implement and manage CCA interventions within the target regions, including members of CTCs and WUGs; ~34
individuals will benefit from strengthened capacities to use the NAP Knowledge Management Platform; and ~720
individuals will be capacitated to adopt sustainable natural resource-based livelihoods.

=== PAGE 33 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 29
D
4. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA
This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the
GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.
D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Direct beneficiaries: defined as the ~85,229 people (40,877 males and 44,352 females) living within the twelve priority
communes across the four target hubs, who will directly experience improved protection against sand inundation,
enhanced access to markets and income-generating opportunities, and increased access to clean water via communal
water points. The total number of direct beneficiaries represents ~1.8% of Mauritania’s total population and ~7% of the
population in the four target wilayahs. Please refer to Table 1 in Annex 23 for a detailed breakdown of direct
beneficiaries.
Indirect beneficiaries: Those who benefit from the project outcomes indirectly through improved environmental
conditions, policy changes or enhanced institutional capacities. This project estimates ~145,008 indirect beneficiaries
(66,519 males and 78,489 females), representing individuals in surrounding or non-priority communes who will
experience knowledge sharing benefits, improved coordination at national and regional levels and access to the small
grants facility for scaling climate change adaptation interventions. This estimate represents ~3% of the country’s total
population and ~12% of the population in all four target wilayahs37. Please refer to Table 2 in Annex 23 for a detailed
breakdown of indirect beneficiaries.
Contribution to GCF Results Areas
The proposed project will contribute to three GCF climate change resilience results areas, namely: i) ARA1 — increased
resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities; ii) ARA2 — increased health, well-being, food and water
security; and iii) ARA4 — increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services. Specific contributions to these
result areas are described below.
ARA1 — Increased resilience of the most vulnerable people and communities.
Under Output 2.1, all individuals in the target hubs will gain enhanced protection from the escalating impacts of sand
inundation. This will be achieved through enhanced ecosystem services generated by EbA and measures for physical
dune fixation, including natural ecosystem restoration and conservation. The ecosystem services include dune fixation,
improved soil stability and reduced airborne dust by establishing of natural windbreaks. EbA solutions will be
complemented by installing physical structures (fencing) around dune fixation sites established under Output 2.1.
Approximately 240 local community members will be given training on installing and maintaining these nature-based
and physical structures, fostering resilience through ownership of these solutions.
Output 2.2 includes several activities that will increase community livelihood resilience to the impacts of climate change
while reducing the anthropogenic landscape degradation, which increases ecosystems’ and communities’ exposure to
desertification. Under this output: i) 500 individuals will benefit from training on climate-resilient and sustainable
livestock-management practices; ii) 500 individuals will receive training and inputs (such as equipment and crop seeds)
for climate-resilient crop agriculture; and iii) 500 individuals will be given technical support in adopting alternative,
natural resource-based livelihoods.
ARA2 — Increased health, well-being, food and water security.
Under Activity 2.2.2, target communities’ resilience to the impacts of drought will be enhanced through the installation
of physical structures such as gabions, percolation tanks and injection wells, which increase aquifer recharge. These
structures will enhance water security for ~85,000 people across the target hubs by increasing groundwater storage
and reducing overland water flow during heavy rainfall events, limiting the amount of water lost through evaporation.
Additionally, surface water flow and access for community members and nomadic pastoralists will be managed through

Population et de l’Habitat (RGPH 2013). Available at: http://www.dgct.mr/wp-content/uploads/2016/06/Populations-2013-brochure-
RGPH_Final-4-aou.pdf

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 30
D
the construction of weirs and dikes. Installing rainwater harvesting structures and communal cisterns will further
enhance water security, directly benefitting all 85,229 individuals across the target communes38,39.
ARA4 — Increased resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services.
The proposed project will improve the resilience to climate change impacts of ~2,123 ha in the target hubs through
ecosystem restoration, conservation and the sustainable management of natural ecosystems. Improved resilience will
be achieved by actively restoring degraded habitats under Activity 2.1.1. The increased health of these managed
ecosystems’ health will, in turn, generate ecosystem services that benefit surrounding communities. These ecosystem
services include, inter alia: i) hydrological regulation; ii) increased biodiversity; iii) protection from sand inundation; iv)
enhanced soil quality; and iv) increased availability of natural resources that support livelihood diversification.
D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The interventions contribute to four paradigm-shifting pathways, as identified in the GCF sectoral guidance, aimed at
enhancing the climate resilience of ecosystems, and the agricultural and water sectors. These pathways include: i)
transformational planning and programming; ii) catalysing climate innovation; iii) mobilising finance at scale; and iv)
establishing coalitions and enhancing knowledge to scale up successful outcomes. Please refer to Table 75 in Annex
2 for a description of each pathway.
Across the four target wilayah covered by the project, the number of communes falling outside of the rural-urban hubs
is as follows: 19 communes in Hodh El Chargui (outside of Nema hub); 22 communes in Hodh El Gharbi (outside of
Tamcheket hub); 7 communes in Tagant (outside of Rachid hub); and lastly, 5 communes in Adrar wilayah (outside of
Aoujeft hub). Collectively, these communes, which are not covered by the project target region, house ~802,600
individuals40. Additionally, in the future, successful project interventions could be replicated in all 8 remaining wilayah
in Mauritania, which collectively house a population of ~2.7 million41.
The mechanisms used for intra-wilayah scaling are equally applicable in scaling up project interventions beyond the
four target wilayah. Spatial scaling will be achieved through mainstreaming climate change and gender considerations
into regional plans, policies and budgets, with support from the DREDDs. Once formally recognised by the Ministry of
the Interior, CTCs can be established in all remaining communes, to further strengthen coordination and knowledge-
sharing, both within and across administrative levels. Newly established CTCs will then use lessons learned and best
practices drawn from the proposed project — access via the NAP Knowledge Management Platform — to implement
and manage successful CCA interventions in communes not covered by the target region. Please refer to Table 51 in
Annex 2 for a detailed list of lessons learned.
Spatial upscaling of ecosystem rehabilitation within the target hubs will be supported by establishing nurseries.
For physical dune fixation, interventions will utilise locally sourced, sustainably collected materials at minimal costs. By
training target community members to install and maintain these interventions, the project will facilitate the scaling up
of these measures across other hubs and areas in the country affected by sand inundation.
Establishing livestock cooperatives will support the expansion of sustainable livestock management. Capacity building
for crop farmers, creating demonstration plots and providing training on climate-resilient seed varietal replication will
help disseminate practices introduced by the project across the target hubs. Improved market access for goods
produced through these livelihood interventions will further incentivise replication of these practices among adjacent
communities, leveraging the benefits and traditional communication systems. Community WUGs will be capacitated
by raising awareness about water supply and demand management practices in the target hubs. Such innovations —
particularly water demand management — are cost-effective and easy to replicate, encouraging their uptake by
neighbouring communities.

D
Creating an enabling environment through transformational planning and programming
An enabling environment for mainstreaming CCA and natural resource management is essential at all institutional
levels to achieve the above upscaling and replication strategies. Accordingly, the proposed project will contribute to
the GCF’s Outcome-level Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional
and regulatory frameworks for low-emission, climate-resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner.
Output 1.1 of the proposed project will contribute to Core Indicator 5 via the establishment of commune-level technical
committees (CTCs). The establishment of WUGs under Output 2.2 will strengthen institutional and regulatory
frameworks for monitoring water use and availability within the target region, while training FBOs and WFS on climate-
smart agriculture will support low-emission, climate-resilient development within the agricultural sector. The
establishment of a Small Grants Facility and associated on-granting mechanism under Output 2.3 of the proposed
project will ensure that systems are put in place to regulate the flow of funds from sustainable livelihood activities back
into CCA beyond the project life cycle.
Potential for scaling up successful climate change adaptation through knowledge sharing
The proposed project will contribute to the GCF’s Outcome-level Core Indicator 8: Degree to which GCF investments
contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes and use of good practices, methodologies and
standards. Numerous studies and assessments will be conducted within the target hubs to improve the country’s
climate change knowledge base, including value chain assessments, surface and groundwater quality and quantity,
and community vulnerability assessments (disaggregated by gender). All the assessments will contribute to a CCA
knowledge management platform, which will be used as a centralised repository accessible to stakeholders across
different administrative levels. Best practices on CCA will be collated, tailored and incorporated into knowledge
products, informing the design and implementation of subsequent adaptation interventions. The platform will: i)
integrate findings from value chain, water resource and vulnerability assessments to create gender-responsive,
location-specific adaptation guidance; ii) document successful interventions from the target hubs that can be replicated
in other wilayah; and iii) feature case studies of successful CCA initiatives from the target hubs, including lessons
learned and implementation challenges. This approach to knowledge management will support both vertical scaling
(enhancing the impact within the target hubs) and horizontal scaling (supporting CCA in other hubs, countries or
regions) of successful adaptation measures. These knowledge products will be integrated into the NAP Knowledge
Management Platform, which manages knowledge related to desertification and climate change in Mauritania.
Financial and Economic modelling carried out for the economic and financial assessment indicates that the adoption
of EbA practises, coupled with dune fixation activities, in each of the hubs could potentially increase crop yields by up
to 30%42 with variations in each hub. Associated increased fodder yields will consequently improve carcass mass of
herd animals by between 11% and 48%, yielding more meat for household consumption43. Rainwater harvesting and
auxiliary infrastructure can also enhance the availability of water to close the gap of unmet water demand in the hubs.
Overall, the economic and financial model yields a benefit to cost ratio of 5.86 to 1. It is also important to note that
scaling of the proposed project is complicated to the spatial arrangements of oasis-based agriculture. There is however
scope for replicability of the project in regions with a similar socio-economic, climatic and ecological conditions.
Replicability could be executed through extension of the proposed grant funding mechanism (Activity 2.3.2), beyond
the GCF funding cycle.
D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The project will contribute towards achieving 11 of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These goals are:
SDG 2 — Zero hunger; SDG 3 — Good health and well-being; SDG 5 — Gender equality; SDG 6 — Clean water and
sanitation; SDG 8 — Decent work and economic growth; SDG 10 — Reducing inequality; SDG 11 — Sustainable cities
and communities; SDG 12 — Responsible consumption and production; SDG 13 — Climate action; SDG 15 — Life
on land; and SDG 16 — Peace, justice and strong institutions. In addition, project interventions will produce numerous
environmental, social and economic co-benefits through a gender-sensitive and -responsive development approach,
as described below.
The proposed project will provide numerous environmental, social and economic co-benefits through the: i)
rehabilitation and conservation of natural ecosystems; ii) introduction of physical structures to reduce sand inundation;
and iii) promotion of climate-resilient livelihoods. The co-benefits are presented below in Table 23.

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Mauritania is classified as a least-developed country, with a gross domestic product (GDP) of ~US$7.9 billion and a
GDP per capita of ~US$1,70044. As a result of high poverty rates, particularly within rural areas, the population is
heavily dependent on agriculture for subsistence; therefore, most households’ capacities to adopt alternative
livelihoods are limited. The country’s Human Capital Index (HCI) score of 38, which ranks it 150th out of 157 countries,
indicates that a child born in Mauritania can only expect to attain 38% of human capital by the age of 18, even if they

&country=MRT

D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | 
 | Mauritania is classified as a least-developed country, with a gross domestic product (GDP) of ~US$7.9 billion and a |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  | 
 | GDP per capita of ~US$1,70044. As a result of high poverty rates, particularly within rural areas, the population is |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |  |

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 33
D
receive complete health and education45. Moreover, despite progress made over the past three decades, Mauritania’s
HDI value places it 157th out of 189 countries (2019), categorising it as having ‘low human development’46 .
In 2019, Mauritania was ranked the 20th country most vulnerable to climate change and other global challenges,
according to the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) country index. With the highest global sensitivity
score (0.602), Mauritania faces substantial insecurity in both the food and water sectors47. Out of 182 countries on the
ND-GAIN readiness index, Mauritania was ranked as the 75th least ready to respond to climate change and economic
shocks, with education and control of corruption being its worst-scoring readiness indicators48.
Between 1900 and 2018, natural hazards such as droughts, disease epidemics, insect infestations and storms (strong
winds and heavy rainfall) impacted more than 11.5 million people, according to the Emergency Events database (EM-
Dat). Between 1990 and 2020, an average of three storms, six epidemics, eight droughts, and 19 floods have been
recorded across Mauritania each year. In 2019, ~33,600 Mauritanians were impacted by floods, while in 2020,
approximately 609,000 people were impacted by droughts49. Flooding alone caused an estimated US$5.5–11.4 billion
in damages and losses between 2000 and 200850. The annual cost of natural capital51 degradation due to climate
change and unsustainable anthropogenic activities has been estimated at ~5.4% of GDP. Under future climate change
scenarios, Mauritania faces substantial challenges such as: i) an overall decrease in water availability of 10–15%; ii)
an increase in land degradation and erosion; iii) the southward expansion of the Sahara Desert, resulting in a decrease
in arable land; iv) a decrease in rangeland availability for livestock; and v) a reduction in food availability52.
Consequently, the impacts of climate change on food and water security, economic growth and human health are
projected to intensify in the country.
The country imports 70–85% of its food requirements each year (accounting for 21% of GDP), despite agriculture being
the third largest economic sector. The cumulative impact of these factors inhibits the capacity of national and local
decision-makers to implement long-term strategies for responding to the adverse impacts of climate change on the
country’s population.
The country’s high public and external debt — estimated at 79% of GDP and 68% of GDP, respectively — constrain
the government’s ability to allocate funding from the national budget for CCA. Moreover, limited opportunities exist for
public and private sector stakeholders to access financial resources for CCA, which is expected to cost ~USD$9.3
billion53 between 2015 and 203054. Given that this cost is likely to increase considerably under future climate change
conditions, external investment is crucial to enhance the climate resilience of Mauritania’s population.
D.5. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The proposed project is well aligned with Mauritania’s national adaptation priorities; it was designed in collaboration
with project stakeholders at all levels.
Mauritania’s First Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), updated in 2021, identifies 19 objectives to be achieved
by 2030. The project directly addresses five of these climate ambitions, namely: i) restoring natural grasslands; ii)
conducting hydrological assessments and water resources availability analyses; iii) strengthening ecosystems’
resilience against climate change; iv) enhancing vulnerable populations’ resilience to climate change, particularly in

 | The proposed project is well aligned with Mauritania’s national adaptation priorities; it was designed in collaboration |

 |  |

=== PAGE 38 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 34
D
rural areas; and v) strengthening institutional and technical capacities at local, regional and national levels to enable
more effective planning, financing and implementation of adaptation measures55.
The project’s objectives are consistent with the country’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA), developed
in 2004, which encourages a cross-sectoral approach to achieving 27 Priority Adaptation Measures (PAMs) for
adaptation to climate change. 19 of the 27 policies and measures are expected to be addressed: eight under Output
2.1 (EbA interventions and physical measures for reducing desertification) and a further 11 under Output 2.2
(sustainable land and natural resources management and improved livelihoods). The most important PAMs to which
the project will directly contribute include: i) enhancing knowledge of surface water’s hydrological cycle; ii) constructing
flooding breakdown dikes in pluvial and oasis zones; iii) promoting water-saving techniques in oasis zones; iv)
participatory reforestation for agroforestry in agricultural zones; and v) re-organising and capacity-building in
communities adversely affected by climate change56.
The project is complementary to the country’s ongoing GCF-supported NAP development process, initiated in 2018.
The project will build on the following NAP activities: i) enhancing the capacity of policymakers and staff from relevant
government institutions (Activity 1.1.1); ii) enhancing the capacity of vulnerable groups, in collaboration with key
ministries (Activity 1.1.1); and iii) developing and implementing at least one awareness-raising campaign targeting
local authorities in each wilayah, civil society organisations and local communities.
The proposed project aligns with Mauritania’s national spatial planning strategy, which aims to strengthen resilience
using an oasis-centred administrative structure. This approach aims to prevent rural exodus and enhance resilience
to the impacts of climate change-induced desertification.
Stakeholder engagement
The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultations with Mauritanian government
representatives, national consultants, public authorities, civil societies, local community members and the private
sector. Mauritania's NDA, the Ministry of the Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD), has been actively
involved in the project's development since inception by contributing to the consultation process and providing inputs
during field missions. Additionally, as the executing entity, UNEP has facilitated dialogue between national consultants,
the MEDD, other relevant institutions and community stakeholders, ensuring that the project responds to the adaptation
priorities of the country. Moreover, the proposed adaptation interventions under this project have been validated by all
relevant project stakeholders, including the NDA.
Community consultations were organised by UNEP within each of the proposed project’s target hubs, namely Aoujeft,
Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema. Specific details regarding, inter alia, the dates of consultations, types of stakeholders
in attendance and number of community members consulted are provided in Annex 7: Summary of Consultations and
Stakeholder Engagement Plan. Four field missions were conducted over a two-year period, during which more than
330 local community members were consulted across the target hubs.
D.6. Efficiency and effectiveness (max`. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Cost-effectiveness of project interventions
A financial and economic analysis (FEA) was conducted to inform this decision-making57. This analysis determined
the costs and benefits associated with: i) implementing EbA interventions, including dune fixation and land
rehabilitation activities, to enhance communities’ resilience to climate change-induced desertification; ii) facilitating
sustainable natural resource management practices and the adoption of sustainable, diversified livelihoods by
developing value chains; and iii) supporting the GoM in developing local, regional and national frameworks for
mainstreaming climate change and gender-responsive adaptation solutions into plans and policies.

=== PAGE 39 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35
D
The economic analysis estimated the net present value58 (NPV) of the net benefits (economic benefits less project
costs) to be ~USD152 million (discounted at social discount rate of 2%59 over 20 years), with a benefit-to-cost ratio of
~5.8 (USD5.86 generated per USD1 spent), reflecting a cost-effective investment for the GCF and GoM.
Project benefits included in the economic analysis are:
• Food crops sustained for household consumption;
• Water security sustained for household consumption;
• Fruit and beverage resources sustained for household consumption;
• Livestock grazing forage sustained for household consumption;
• Agricultural income generation sustained for trading households;
• Natural products income generation sustained for trading households; and
• Income generation opportunities created through new goods and services for trading households.
Health benefits
The NPV of health benefits sustained under the proposed GCF project is estimated at USD24.3 million. This estimate
was determined by conducting a literature review to reveal the incremental healthcare cost for treating malnourished
patients. The healthcare cost per capita in Mauritania was estimated using available data and the incremental cost
was applied. The proposition behind this is that by implementing the GCF interventions, the livelihoods of the population
in the target hubs improve, resulting in less malnourishment in hub communities. This leads to substantial cost savings,
as literature suggests it is more expensive to treat patients who are/were malnourished. . Further details are available
in Annex 3: Financial and Economic Appraisal.
Rural livelihood benefits
A financial analysis of income potential sustained by the adoption of climate-smart agriculture, climate-resilient
livestock management and alternative, natural resource-based livelihoods was carried out using best available data
and research. Table 5 below provides a summary of the financial and economic results.

NPV of Health Benefit $ 24,337,247 1,458,515 5,500,464 4,617,809 12,760,459
NPV of Total Economic &
Ecosystem Service Benefit $ 182,687,481 23,370,505 52,588,172 30,306,107 76,422,699

Financial IRR % 22%
ERR % 29%
Benefit-to-cost Ratio % 5.86
This requested GCF grant investment will be supported by in-kind co-financing of US$2.657 million from the MEDD
and regional authorities in the target hubs. Co-finance from the MEDD and regional authorities will be used to cover
baseline costs, including operations and maintenance (which may be revised during project implementation) necessary
to ensure sustainable land and water resources management. With the support of GCF grant funding, the proposed
project will deliver several adaptation benefits using an integrated approach to EbA and sustainable natural resources
management, contributing to a paradigm shift in the country’s approach to addressing the impacts of climate change-
induced desertification61.
Beyond the direct co-financing, the proposed project is strategically positioned to benefit from parallel financing totalling
~US$43.8 million, drawn from complementary initiatives within the target regions. These include the Integrated
Management of Protected Areas in the Arid Regions of Mauritania (IMPADRA) with around US$20 million, the
Sustainable Management of Natural Resources project (PROGRES) with an estimated US$6.6 million, the Africa
Integrated Climate Risk Management Programme with approximately US$14.7 million, and a climate change
adaptation project with around US$6 million in funding62. The combined investment of ~US$63.8 million will be used
efficiently and effectively to achieve two main project objectives. First, local, regional and national institutions will gain
improved access to climate-risk information for strategic land-use planning and natural resource management that will,
in turn, facilitate continued investment in ecosystem restoration, EbA, diversified livelihoods and sustainable natural
resources management. Second, communities in rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary will have
sustainable access to food and water resources, reducing pressure from the impacts of climate change-induced
desertification.
61 A detailed description of project activities is presented in Sections B.3 and E.6 of the Funding Proposal.
62 Please refer to Table 70 in Annex 2 for a detailed list of parallel finance opportunities.

Sustainability | Nationally, public investment in CCA includes the
Presidential Priority Program (PPP). This investment,
however, is currently limited to less than 5% of the
country’s annual GDP. At the wilayah-level regional
funds for sustainable development also exist, but these
vary considerably from year to year, making long-term
planning for CCA investment difficult. Moreover, there
are limited guidelines and policies dictating what
percentage of this investment should be channelled
towards CCA.
Private sector investment has somewhat alleviated
gaps in public CCA investment. This investment
includes PES schemes introduced to some areas by
the World Bank and IFAD. These PES schemes,
however, are limited in their physical extents. | Low | The project will create a
strengthened enabling
environment that will support
the long-term sustainability and
scaling of CCA measures in
Mauritania. This enabling
environment will include:
• improved vertical (across
national, regional and local
levels) and horizontal
(between government
departments at the hub
level) coordination
mechanisms between
government institutions at
the local, regional and
national levels through | Mainstreaming adaptation into regional plans, policies and
budgets and the formal recognition of CTCs established under
the project will make it more likely that community participatory
processes will be supported and continued.
The continued operation and maintenance of the proposed
project’s EbA interventions will be promoted by the
implementation of the on-granting mechanism.
Skills development and retention by: i) institutionalising
training programmes; ii) embedding CCA and sustainable
natural resource management practices into standard
operating procedures; and iii) fostering a culture of continuous
learning and adaptation within participating organisations.
~1,800 national and sub-national decision-makers will be
trained on how to design, implement and manage CCA
interventions within the target regions, including members of
CTCs and WUGs; ~34 individuals will benefit from

To ensure long-term adoption, extension services and
capacity-building programmes will be provided. The project
will also address the challenges of incentivising new
technologies by considering local needs and promoting
beneficial, locally-aligned practices. Community involvement
will be prioritised through participatory design, implementation
and management. Continuous monitoring and evaluation will
inform adaptive management and ensure project outcomes.
By incorporating these lessons and best practices, this
project aims to create a resilient and sustainable oasis
ecosystem that provides long-term benefits for local
communities.

pathways in a
country-driven
manner | Mauritania has made some progress towards
addressing its resilience to climate change
impacts through alignment with several
multilateral environmental agreements —
including the UNFCCC, CBD and CCD65 — as
well as several national planning frameworks
and strategies. Despite these efforts, there are
still considerable gaps in the policy and
institutional environments at the national,
regional and local levels regarding climate
change and natural resource management.
Climate change knowledge in general has not
been fully institutionalised across Mauritania.
Government officials at both the provincial and
local levels often do not fully understand the
impacts of climate change and how to effectively
develop strategies to improve resilience.
Additionally, staff from key sectoral
organisations at the local level often have limited
technical capacity to support CCA initiatives. | low |  |  | • Create clearer mandates for
both state and non-state
actors regarding integrating
CCA into sub-national and
local legislation, policy and
improved adaptation funding.

use of knowledge on climate
change impacts and
adaptation responses,
bridging the current gap
between research and policy-
making.
• Improved incorporation and
mainstreaming of climate
change adaptation and natural
resource management into
regional and local budgets,
strategies and plans.
• Robust regional and local
coordination mechanisms for
implementing climate change
adaptation.

E.5. Project/programme specific indicators (project outcomes and outputs)

--- Table 1 on Page 49 ---
 |  |  | climate change adaptation
activities. |  | 
Core indicator 8:
Degree to which GCF
investments
contribute to effective
knowledge generation
and learning
processes, and use of
good practices,
methodologies and
standards | There are limited assessments on the impact of
climate change and vulnerabilities of
communities and ecosystems throughout most
of Mauritania and specifically in the four target
hubs. Information on losses and damages from
climatic hazards, for example, are not quantified
and readily available. Although some lessons
learned have been generated from previous
adaptation projects, there is generally limited
knowledge on the implementation and benefits
of CCA measures and natural resource
management. Economic appraisals of potential
cost-effective adaptation measures such as
integrated land rehabilitation, water resource
management, resilient crop species and
livestock breeds have not been undertaken in
Mauritania that would inform and promote the
upscaling of these interventions. | low | Enhanced access of decision-
makers to relevant tools,
knowledge and data on climate
change impacts and appropriate
cost-effective adaptation
responses. Removal of the
current gap between research
and policymaking on CCA to
enable effective and informed
responses to the impacts of
climate change across the
country. | Through the proposed project, several
studies and assessments will be
undertaken to improve the knowledge
base on climate change impacts and
the viability of sustainable adaptation
interventions.
Data from assessments, along with
lessons learned and best practices
from the proposed project, will be
stored, analysed and shared through
the NAP Knowledge Management
Platform (Output 1.1). Using the
platform developed under the
National Adaptation Plan, trained
CTC members and government staff
will systematically document policy
findings, coordination mechanisms
and methods for integrating climate
considerations into local plans and
budgets. This documentation will
support the replication of successful
approaches beyond the initial 12
communes.
Communities will be capacitated to
install, implement and maintain
adopted interventions or practices on
dune fixation, restoration, climate-
smart agriculture and water use
management through training
programmes, workshops and
awareness-raising campaigns. | Multiple
sub-national
areas within
a country

Activity 2.1.1 will additionally promote the
implementation of climate-resilient sustainable
livestock management practices to assist with
the protection of degraded ecosystems against
over-grazing. This will include the replacement of
local livestock breeds with climate-resilient
breeds, the growth of climate-resilient fodder
varieties for supplementary feeding during the
lean season and the establishment of ‘set-aside’
rotational grazing systems to rehabilitate
degraded ecosystems and rangelands.

E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
A full time Monitoring Officer will be employed to conduct and coordinate monitoring and evaluation (M&E) activities for the project. The Monitoring Officer will
design and operationalise a performance monitoring framework to track the project’s progress towards achieving its targets. This will include: i) measuring
performance against the project indicators (Sections E.3–5) to evaluate the progress of the project; ii) reporting the project’s performance to the PSC and PMU;
and iii) providing technical support to the PC. Additionally, the Monitoring Officer will be responsible for overseeing and monitoring the application of gender-
disaggregated indicators and ensuring that all gender targets are met. At particular milestones throughout the project — including annual performance reports,
interim evaluations and at project conclusion — the project team will undertake evidence-gathering exercises to verify the progress. These internal reports will be
further validated by reviews at the interim and end of the project implementation, as described below. UNEP will be responsible for managing the interim and the
final evaluation of the project.
Interim Evaluation
The UNEP Task Manager will oversee the process of hiring an external independent consultant to carry out the interim evaluation, who will provide an
assessment of project performance against its targets at the project’s mid-point. This will be a formative exercise and will include analysing whether the project is
on track, what problems and challenges the project is encountering, and what corrective actions are required so that the project will achieve its intended
outcomes by project completion in the most efficient and sustainable way. The PSC will participate in the interim evaluation and develop a management response
to the evaluation recommendations along with an implementation plan. It is the responsibility of the UNEP Task Manager to monitor whether the agreed
recommendations are being implemented during the remainder of the project’s operational life.
Final Evaluation
An independent consultant will be contracted by the UNEP Evaluation Office to conduct the final evaluation at the end of project implementation. UNEP‘s
Evaluation Office (EO) will be responsible for overseeing the final evaluation at the end of project implementation — which is a summative evaluation – and will
liaise with the UNEP Task Manager throughout the process. An independent assessment of project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g.,
strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and sustainability) will be made based on documentary evidence, stakeholder interviews and, in
most cases, a field mission. The evaluation criteria will be aligned with the criteria contained in the GCF Evaluation Policy. Each evaluation criterion will be rated
using a six-point rating scheme and a weighted average will be determined to provide an overall performance rating for the project as a whole. Where there are
any differences in ratings between the evaluation team and the Evaluation Office, a final determination will be made by the Evaluation Office when the evaluation
report is finalised.

The draft final evaluation report will be sent to project stakeholders during a commenting process managed by the Evaluation Office. Formal comments on the
report will be shared by the Evaluation Office in an open and transparent manner. This evaluation report will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a

PMU will consolidate the reports and submit them to UNEP as the AE. In turn, UNEP will submit annual performance reports and semi-annual financial reports to
GCF. The detailed reporting timelines are as follows:
Under the PCAs, the EE will be required to report to UNEP as follows:
a. Progress reports: by 30 July for January to June;
b. Annual Performance Reports on or before the start of second quarter (Q2) of each year, starting from year 2;
c. Quarterly financial reports by 15 January, 15 April, 15 July, and 15 October;
d. Annual audited statements by 30 April;
e. Final report: within 3 months of Project completion.
UNEP (AE) reports to the GCF:
a. Annual Performance Reports by 1 March;
b. Semi-annual Financial Information by 1 March and 30 September;
c. Interim Evaluation report: halfway through Programme;
d. Final APR: within 6 months of Project completion;
e. Final Evaluation report: within 12 months of Project completion.
Monitoring will also be undertaken by the AE through supervision visits and field missions to track implementation progress and challenges and strategically plan
the way forward.

6. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT
F.1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)
Selected Risk Factor 1
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Medium
Description
High staff turnover within government institutions could impede capacity building, the retention of skills and
knowledge, and knowledge-sharing across relevant institutions.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Communities and local authorities at different levels will be involved in the implementation of the proposed project. This
will include the establishment of hub-level coordinating platforms, community-level decision-making groups, livestock
cooperatives and community-level water-user groups through which information on — and ownership of — project
interventions will be shared and maintained. As a result, knowledge regarding project planning and implementation
processes will be spread across a range of individuals, thereby reducing the impact on the project if individuals leave
their respective organisations.
Under the proposed project, institutional stability will be provided by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) established
as part of the project management arrangements (see Section B.4 for more detail).
Selected Risk Factor 2
Category Probability Impact
Technical and operational Medium Medium
Description
Climate-related hazards such as droughts, floods or sandstorms or other environmental hazards may disrupt or
damage nature-based or physical structures aimed at increasing community resilience to climate change during their
implementation. The survival of planted seedlings used for agricultural practices or ecosystem restoration is also
likely to be threatened by climate threats such as drought as well as pests such as herbivorous mammals or insects.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Project interventions will be closely and continuously monitored to identify potential climatic and environmental hazards
so that appropriate measures can be enacted should such an event occur. Agricultural planting and ecosystem
restoration will be undertaken using drought-resilient species and will be undertaken during the cooler months. Water-
efficient irrigation techniques such as bottomless cylinders and the use of mulch to reduce evaporation will also be
considered to maintain seedling health. Where needed, supplementary planting of seedlings will be undertaken to
replace lost plants. Enclosures and guards will be utilised to protect restored areas from herbivorous pests, with guards
specifically aimed at prevent animal invasion and identifying any damages to the enclosures.
Selected Risk Factor 3
Category Probability Impact
Governance Medium Medium
Description
Limited coordination between implementing partners reducing the efficiency of interventions.
Mitigation Measure(s)
A project management unit and steering committee will be established to coordinate project implementation (Further
details on the implementation arrangements are presented in Section C.7). At the hub level, coordination and
management committees will ensure coordination among government institutions. At the community-level, user groups,
decision-making groups and cooperatives will ensure coordination between communities and local authorities.

The relevant implementing partners that were involved in the design of the project activities and have agreed on all

Selected Risk Factor 4
Category Probability Impact
Governance Medium Medium
Description
Limited participation by women and other marginalised groups in project interventions and decision-making could lead
to the needs of these groups not being considered or represented in project design or implementation. In particular,
similar previous projects have shown that limited female participation in formal training activities is a risk as a result of
long travel distances and limited transport options for women.
Mitigation Measure(s)
During project development, women and marginalized groups were consulted to ensure that their specific requirements
are addressed. A GAP analysis was undertaken to ensure that interventions are gender-sensitive and that women and
marginalized groups are represented in awareness-raising, training and other measures that benefit communities.
Additionally, all members of the project management structures will receive gender sensitivity training to ensure that
decisions that affect project implementation are socially and gender sensitive. Particularly for training activities, formal
training workshops will be organised on-site to reduce the need for women to travel.
Selected Risk Factor 5
Category Probability Impact
Governance Medium
Description
Changes in political leadership at local level, as well as within ministries and other participating governmental
institutions could result in delays in implementation.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Even with more frequent changes in governing parties through elections and via personnel shifts in ministries, which
are to be anticipated, the project will be fully anchored through the involvement of multiple ministries and institutions,
ensuring project continuity without major interruptions. Specific mitigation measures which have been built into the
projects design are mentioned below.
• Regular engagement with and capacity building of non-political actors — such as government agency officials, staff
and local community members — will be prioritised during project implementation.
• Formulation of local ordinances (including community-level user groups, decision-making groups and
cooperatives).
• Mainstreaming of project interventions into local planning, policy-making and budgeting systems will ensure that
political challenges will have negligible impact on the sustainability of project interventions.
Selected Risk Factor 6
Category Probability Impact
Other Low Medium
Description
Global shocks such as the Covid-19 pandemic or regional conflicts could influence government priorities, resulting in
delays in project implementation and reducing the likeliness of project sustainability.
Mitigation Measure(s)

While the project will not prevent possible global shocks, implementation arrangements developed for the project that
consists of multiple responsible parties at different responsibility levels, co-finance commitments and a detailed
workplan will assist in ensuring project activities are met. The experience of the AE and international best practice on
mitigating the effects of global shocks — including lessons learned from the Covid-19 pandemic — will be drawn on to
inform the response of the PSC and implementing partners to similar shocks if they were to occur.
Selected Risk Factor 7
Category Probability Impact
Other Medium Medium
Description
Resistance by water users, land holders and local communities to adaptation measures, alternative livelihoods and
climate-resilient agricultural practices may result in on-the-ground interventions not being effective or sustainable.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Consultations with community stakeholders — including water users, land holders, farmers and pastoralists — during
project development included raising awareness on the proposed interventions to address drought and sand inundation.
These consultations additionally aimed to gain support and buy-in from the communities by participating with them to
identify their specific vulnerabilities and adaptation preferences. Details on these consultations are presented in Annex
7: Stakeholder consultations and engagement plan.
During project implementation, awareness-raising campaigns on climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustainable
natural resource management will encourage compliance for these interventions through the sharing of information on
their benefits and by fostering ownership of the project by local stakeholders. Adequate training on the introduced
methods will also be provided to ensure that beneficiaries are able to implement them effectively. Additionally,
community-level user groups, decision-making groups and cooperatives within each target hub will ensure that local
stakeholders (including local communities) are represented at both decision-making and implementation levels. This
will ensure that their concerns are considered and that they are not excluded from accessing benefits, promoting
compliance with the adaptation measures implemented by the project.
Selected Risk Factor 8
Category Probability Impact
Other Medium Medium
Description
Selected sites for on-the-ground interventions are no chosen effectively and do not address the requirements of
vulnerable communities, or the distance between sites makes project implementation difficult.
Mitigation Measure(s)
Site selection within the target areas has been undertaken during project development using international and national
expertise and through participatory engagements with local stakeholders. Risk assessments and a SWAT analysis
undertaken during the project design phase have additionally contributed to an informed selection of the sites, which
have undergone a detailed multi-criteria analysis agreed upon by major stakeholders involved in project development.
If sites are subsequently found to be unviable or undesirable during project implementation, coordination between the
PMU and Implementing Partners will assist in guiding a flexible and iterative response to identifying alternative sites.
The cost relating to the large travel distances between sites has been accounted for in the project’s budget. Where
possible, local implementing representatives will be used to coordinate the day-to-day implementation of the
interventions to reduce the need for unnecessary travel between sites.
