7. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
Project categorisation
The environmental and social risks associated with this project have been evaluated in accordance with the GCF’s
Environmental and Social Safeguards. The proposed project is classified as Category B (moderate risk), based on the
screening process and regulations used by the GCF. Three of the 8 safeguard categories are deemed moderate risk:
SS 1: Biodiversity, Ecosystems and Sustainable Natural Resource Management: The inclusion of Prosopis Juliflora
as a potential species for biological dune-fixation is considered to be necessary risk within very specific contexts (such
as highly mobile dune environments where indigenous species are not able to establish). However, there is a high risk
of proliferation, and the potential difficulty of removing these plants is well established.
SS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security: There are some health and safety risks inherent to undertaking
construction activities, including for dune fixation and water infrastructure installation. These risks apply to project
personnel, contracted labour as well community members residing in, contributing to, or traveling through, the vicinity
of the construction activities. There are also some minor risks that rainwater harvesting and other water provisioning
infrastructure could pose a health and safety risk to community members, related to the transmission of vector-or-
water-borne diseases that are associated with standing water or poor hygiene practices.
SS 8: Labor and working conditions: gender inequality is prevalent in Mauritania. While the project does include a
Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP), there may be certain instances where women will be inhibited
from participating in specific employment opportunities due to cultural or social prohibitions.
Justification for this classification is provided in Annex 6: Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which
contains an overview of the general project implementation risks that have been identified to date. A detailed risk
rating for each activity is provided in Chapter 5 of Annex 6.
Stakeholder consultation
To date, extensive stakeholder consultations have informed the project preparation process. The full Stakeholder
Engagement Plan is presented in Annex 7: Stakeholder Engagement Plan, which includes the full list of stakeholders
consulted and a summary of their feedback. The results of stakeholder consultations have closely informed the design
of the proposed project and development of the ESMP. These consultations are expected to continue during the
implementation phase, to ensure regular communication between project personnel and affected communities.
Accordingly, a participatory approach will be used when conducting E&S risk screening exercises, such that local
community members’ input is incorporated into activity-level risk registries and associated risk management
strategies. As a minimum, it is recommended that specific Stakeholder Engagement Plans be developed for each
project Outcome, since each activity set will have different consultation requirements, stakeholders and E&S risks.
Project grievance redress mechanism
A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) has been developed for this project and are described in detail in Chapter
10 of the ESMP (Annex 6). The proposed mechanism is compatible with Mauritanian legislation and compliant with
UNEP and GCF standards, enabling any project stakeholders with complaints or grievances related to the project to
communicate their concerns through an appropriate process. The GRM will be available to stakeholders at all stages
of the project and will provide an accessible, rapid, fair and effective response to concerned community members —
including any vulnerable groups that may lack access to formal legal systems or support. The GCF Independent
Redress Mechanism will also be available to stakeholders and affected persons who have grievances or issues
relevant to the project’s activities.
All project grievances (both internal and external) will be resolved using the steps below, with the hub-level CTCs
assuming most of the duties and responsibilities in this regard:
Step 1. Receipt of complaint/grievance;
Step 2. Acknowledgement of complaint/grievance;
Step 3. Assessment, investigation and resolution of complaint/grievance;
Step 4. Closure of investigation and redress process; and
Step 5. Outcome of the corrective action is verified with the complainant.

 | GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS | 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages) |  | 
Project categorisation
The environmental and social risks associated with this project have been evaluated in accordance with the GCF’s
Environmental and Social Safeguards. The proposed project is classified as Category B (moderate risk), based on the
screening process and regulations used by the GCF. Three of the 8 safeguard categories are deemed moderate risk:
SS 1: Biodiversity, Ecosystems and Sustainable Natural Resource Management: The inclusion of Prosopis Juliflora
as a potential species for biological dune-fixation is considered to be necessary risk within very specific contexts (such
as highly mobile dune environments where indigenous species are not able to establish). However, there is a high risk
of proliferation, and the potential difficulty of removing these plants is well established.
SS 4: Community Health, Safety and Security: There are some health and safety risks inherent to undertaking
construction activities, including for dune fixation and water infrastructure installation. These risks apply to project
personnel, contracted labour as well community members residing in, contributing to, or traveling through, the vicinity
of the construction activities. There are also some minor risks that rainwater harvesting and other water provisioning
infrastructure could pose a health and safety risk to community members, related to the transmission of vector-or-
water-borne diseases that are associated with standing water or poor hygiene practices.
SS 8: Labor and working conditions: gender inequality is prevalent in Mauritania. While the project does include a
Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP), there may be certain instances where women will be inhibited
from participating in specific employment opportunities due to cultural or social prohibitions.
Justification for this classification is provided in Annex 6: Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP), which
contains an overview of the general project implementation risks that have been identified to date. A detailed risk
rating for each activity is provided in Chapter 5 of Annex 6.
Stakeholder consultation
To date, extensive stakeholder consultations have informed the project preparation process. The full Stakeholder
Engagement Plan is presented in Annex 7: Stakeholder Engagement Plan, which includes the full list of stakeholders
consulted and a summary of their feedback. The results of stakeholder consultations have closely informed the design
of the proposed project and development of the ESMP. These consultations are expected to continue during the
implementation phase, to ensure regular communication between project personnel and affected communities.
Accordingly, a participatory approach will be used when conducting E&S risk screening exercises, such that local
community members’ input is incorporated into activity-level risk registries and associated risk management
strategies. As a minimum, it is recommended that specific Stakeholder Engagement Plans be developed for each
project Outcome, since each activity set will have different consultation requirements, stakeholders and E&S risks.
Project grievance redress mechanism
A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) has been developed for this project and are described in detail in Chapter
10 of the ESMP (Annex 6). The proposed mechanism is compatible with Mauritanian legislation and compliant with
UNEP and GCF standards, enabling any project stakeholders with complaints or grievances related to the project to
communicate their concerns through an appropriate process. The GRM will be available to stakeholders at all stages
of the project and will provide an accessible, rapid, fair and effective response to concerned community members —
including any vulnerable groups that may lack access to formal legal systems or support. The GCF Independent
Redress Mechanism will also be available to stakeholders and affected persons who have grievances or issues
relevant to the project’s activities.
All project grievances (both internal and external) will be resolved using the steps below, with the hub-level CTCs
assuming most of the duties and responsibilities in this regard:
Step 1. Receipt of complaint/grievance;
Step 2. Acknowledgement of complaint/grievance;
Step 3. Assessment, investigation and resolution of complaint/grievance;
Step 4. Closure of investigation and redress process; and
Step 5. Outcome of the corrective action is verified with the complainant. |  |

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
A Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) for this project provides an overview of gender-disaggregated
challenges, specifically those related to: i) women’s rights; ii) the gendered division of labour; iii) political participation;
v) access to land; vi) access to justice; vii) gender-based violence (GBV); and vii) climate change impacts.
Among the proposed project’s target population, climate change disproportionately affects women and other socio-
economically marginalised groups in Mauritania as a result of unequal access to productive resources75,76. Although
Mauritania has ratified the International Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women
(CEDAW)77, the GoM has not yet to lift reservations applying to articles 13(a) and 16 of CEDAW, which relate to: i)
eliminating discrimination in family benefits; and ii) requiring equality in marriage and family matters.
Gender inequality is apparent at the household level, with the country's Personal Status Code (PSC) established in
2001, serving as the primary codified law governing marriage and family relations in Mauritania78. The PSC provides
a marital framework based on male guardianship over women and children, where men are considered the lawful
heads of their respective households. However, ~50% of households in the focal hubs of Aoujeft, Rachid and
Tamcheket are headed by women, mainly resulting from divorce, widowhood, and male migration to urban areas for
work79. Statistics detailing the gender of household heads in Nema are not currently available. Additionally, the PSC
states that men hold decision-making authority, while women are expected to assist in managing the family.
Mauritania’s national Labour Code reinforces these conventional gender roles by prohibiting women from working in
occupations considered dangerous, arduous or immoral80. Although the GoM has ratified International Labour
Organisation conventions on discrimination and equal remuneration in the workplace, women's labour force
participation remains low (31%81), and they face a considerable gender pay gap82.
Educational disparities exist, with lower primary school completion rates for girls (~56%) compared to boys (~64%)83.
Early marriage and childbearing jeopardise girls' chances of completing school, resulting in lower literacy rates for
women (~57%) compared to men (~70%)84. Gender discrepancies in access to finance limit women's capacities to
adapt to climate change. While the Constitution grants equal rights to men and women open bank accounts, access
credit and register businesses, cultural norms and gender roles limit women's participation. Women also face 'time
poverty' as they spend considerable time on non-remunerated household activities limiting their time available to
diversify their livelihoods or participate in projects designed to improve their resilience.
In accordance with the GCF Updated Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan 2020–2023 (GCF/B.24/15), the proposed
project will address gender dimensions within the four target hubs by incorporating the following measures into
activities designed under the proposed project:
• the establishment of a gender-balanced project management unit (PMU);
• the recruitment of a Gender Officer within the PMU, to support and monitor implementation of the GAAP;
• compulsory gender training for the PMU;
• the inclusion of gender-mainstreaming tasks into all relevant consultancy and partnership ToRs;
• the identification and strengthening of at least six women-led civil society organisations (CSOs), to assist with
knowledge dissemination and recruiting women for activities and training workshops;

content/uploads/2019/03/Mauritania-Overview-Table.pdf

=== PAGE 69 ===
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65
F
• specific strategies for facilitating women’s participation in monitoring, evaluation and learning initiatives; and
• strategies for involving women in decision-making processes — particularly those relating to their land, resources,
activities, families and livelihoods — to overcome constraints on access to natural resources.
The project’s approach to addressing gender-related challenges and promoting gender equality are presented in the
Gender Action Plan (Annex 8), with specific gender-sensitive benefits summarised below.
• Training modules targeting men and women, empowering policymakers, decision-makers, and community leaders
to mainstream gender considerations into adaptation planning and implementation (Output 1.1).
• Enhancing women’s leadership capacity though participation in training and skills-building activities, promoting
representation in decision-making on climate adaptation and resource management interventions (Output 1.1).
• Developing gender-responsive policies, including one focused on gender in natural resource management and
land-use planning (Output 1.1), to ensure gender-responsiveness is incorporated into national priorities, planning
and budgets.
• Integrating women into the design and dissemination of awareness campaigns, tailoring core messages so they
are gender-responsive (Output 1.2).
• Including women in the selection of priority sites for rehabilitation and design of awareness-raising campaigns,
addressing unique threats and challenges they face (Output 2.1).
• Empowering women in target communities through training and active participation in developing livelihood
activities, enabling them to independently support themselves and their families (Output 2.3).
• Enabling women to actively participate in decision-making processes relating to their lands, resources, activities,
families and livelihoods (Output 2.3). This will allow them to contribute to directly address impacts that increase
their vulnerability to climate change.
Stakeholder consultations and engagement with women’s CSOs during the project design process aided in identifying
gender-disaggregated climate change impacts within the Mauritanian context. Continued engagement with these
groups during the project implementation stage will further promote gender equality within the proposed project’s
target regions.
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by UN financial regulations, rules and
procedures, as well as UNEP’s Programme Manual. The financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public
Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS), are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulations and Rules of the UN.
Within this context, funding-allocation mechanisms are managed as per the UN framework, including eligibility criteria,
proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited
annually by the UN Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for GCF resources.
The funding of a project will be established through a distinct grant within the Trust Fund, with the project itself being
set up in the UNEP Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) as a ‘Work Breakdown Structure’ (WBS), which is itself
further broken down into ‘Work Breakdown Structure Elements’ (WBSEs), organised by output and outcome. The
grant of the project will be linked to the WBSEs to fund the activities contributing to the delivery of specific outputs (as
per the disbursement schedule detailed in the term sheet). The fee income will be managed through a dedicated grant
independently and separately of the GCF project grant funds. In line with UNEP procedures, the project will appoint a
Financial and Administrative Officer within the PMU who will be responsible for monitoring, reporting on and approving
requests for funds on a quarterly basis for the activities executed by the MEDD. Reports to summarise the
disbursement and projected demands for project funding will be prepared and submitted to a UNEP Task Manager
who will conduct project supervision, in line with reporting standards and methodologies applied in past projects, such
as those implemented using GCF modalities.
The UN financial regulations and rules require the segregation of duties, and safeguards to ensure compliance with
UN financial rules and regulations. In addition, a Fund Management Officer will be appointed to assist UNEP’s Project
Officer with all financial monitoring and supervision functions. All procurement will be undertaken in line with UN
procurement regulations, rules and policies. In accordance with UNEP’s modalities for project implementation, funds
will be transferred in tranches to the EE (MEDD), once the EE has satisfied all conditions defined under the
legal/cooperation instrument — including the Project Cooperation Agreement (PCA), which will be signed between
UNEP’s Climate Change Division and the MEDD. The PCA will include specific obligations for the EE on financial
management and reporting and will require periodic financial reporting to follow international financial and auditing
standards. The PCA specifically requires the audit be undertaken by a recognised firm of certified public accountants

F
or, for governments, by a government auditor. This auditor should state whether the GCF proceeds were covered by
the scope of the audit.
As a GoM authority, the MEDD follows the government’s financial and procurement rules and standards. During the
proposed project’s inception phase, UNEP will undertake a thorough assessment of the MEDD’s capacity to undertake
procurement in line with UN regulations, rules and processes. This assessment will guide the procurement monitoring
plan which will be agreed upon between UNEP and the MEDD. The assessment will be undertaken following project
approval but prior to project implementation. It will include assessments of elements of governance and public
accountability such as, inter alia, review of the existence and quality of policies, legal and institutional framework, and
systems supporting transparency, accountability and controls, especially in the use of donor funds. The outcome of
the assessment will determine the threshold for procurement that is undertaken by the MEDD, which will be reflected
in the procurement monitoring plan. This plan is an annex to the PCA and will be shared with GCF upon signature, as
required by the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA). The procurement monitoring plan will also be reviewed periodically.
The project’s investments in equipment will be undertaken in accordance with UN procurement procedures. Finally,
in line with the UNEP Programme Manual, the MEDD will be mandated to provide an annual compliance audit covering
all aspects of the project execution including review of all expenditures incurred during the financial year.

annexes:
• full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with
an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the
accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and
• redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

information.

Le Directeur ريدملا
To: The Green Climate Fund (“GCF”)
Re : Funding proposal for the GCF by UN Environment programme (UNEP) regarding Strengthening
the resilience of ecosystems and populations in four regional hubs in northern Mauritania.
Dear Madam, Sir,
We refer to the programme titled Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations in
four regional hubs in northern Mauritania as included in the funding proposal submitted by
United nations Environment Programm (UNEP) to us on 01 September 2023.
The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of Sidi Mohamed El wavi, the National
Designated Authority of Mauritania.
Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we
hereby communicate our no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.
By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:
(a) The government of Mauritania has no-objection to the programme as included in the funding
proposal;
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national
priorities, strategies and plans of Mauritania;
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as
included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.
We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the programme as
included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.
We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within
the scope of the programme
We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.
Kind regards,
Sidi Mohamed El Wavi

Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations in
four regional hubs in northern Mauritania
Accredited entity: United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)
Country: Mauritania
Project size: Small

II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment
2.1 Project background

=== PAGE 77 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 77
3.
Climate change is profoundly impacting Mauritanian communities, particularly those
living along the Sahel-Saharan boundary, which extends from the north-west to the south-east
of the country. A noticeable impact of climate change-induced desertification is the accelerated
advance of sand dunes. Arable land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, severely
compromising the agricultural and livestock practices in the oases. In addition, sand inundation
threatens critical socioeconomic infrastructure, including, inter alia, roads, school buildings and
hospitals. In extreme cases, sand dunes have displaced entire villages, in some cases more than
once. Additionally, the declining water table has dried up many pastoral wells, disrupting the
traditional nomadic lifestyles of pastoralists and forcing them to adopt sedentary lifestyles
around rural oases. This shift increases pressure on limited natural resources and heightens the
ri sk of conflicts over resources.
4.
This proposed project focuses on addressing these climate-induced challenges by
adopting an integrated ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approach to restore ecosystems,
prevent desertification, and enhance water and food security. Targeted interventions will be
implemented across four regional hubs – Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema – located in the
country's most climate-impacted regions. This approach includes establishing 2,123 hectares
(ha) of green-grey dune-fixation infrastructure and 120 kilometres of protective fence line to
facilitate the rehabilitation and maintenance of degraded landscapes and enhance ecosystem
services related to dune stabilization and the supply of natural resources. Additionally, the
project will develop or strengthen sustainable agriculture-based livelihoods and enhance water
se curity.
5.
The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 people (40,877 men and 44,352
women) and indirectly benefit 145,008 people (66,519 men and 78,489 women), reaching a
total of 230,237 beneficiaries. The total project financing is USD 33,560,345, of which a GCF
grant of USD 30,902,775 is requested. The Government of Mauritania will provide in-kind co-
financing of USD 2,657,570. The project is submitted under the environmental and social
safeguards Category B, and the total duration of the project is six years.
22.2.2.1. CCoommppoonennetn 1t:- Gboyv-ecronmanpcoe nanedn tC aapnaacliytys-ibsu ilding
Outcome 1: Increased implementation and upscaling of ecosystem-based adaptation measures
across the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania (total cost: USD 2.2 million; GCF cost: USD 2.1
million)
6.
Component 1 will be crucial in laying the foundation for long-term climate resilience
and sustainable land-use practices in the Sahara-Sahel region of Mauritania. The component
aims to integrate EbA approaches into governance and land-use planning, with a particular
focus on strengthening the capacity of regional and local institutions to support climate-resilient
interventions. It will work towards mainstreaming climate adaptation and EbA measures into
go vernment policies and budgets, particularly in vulnerable areas.
7.
A key feature of Component 1 is the establishment of 12 commune-level coordination
and technical committees (CTCs). These CTCs will facilitate knowledge-sharing, natural
resource management, and the implementation of EbA activities across the four target hubs.
Each CTC will be composed of local stakeholders, including community representatives,
technical experts and government officials. These committees will be trained to manage and
upscale climate adaptation measures at the local level, ensuring that decision-making processes
re flect the needs and priorities of local communities.
8.
Additionally, Component 1 will focus on the creation and dissemination of knowledge
products such as training manuals, best practices and policy briefs. These products will aid in

=== PAGE 78 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 78
informed decision-making and the scaling of successful EbA practices, thus enhancing the
resilience of ecosystems and communities. The integration of climate change considerations
into subnational planning processes will be central to ensuring that climate adaptation becomes
a key aspect of local governance, fostering long-term sustainability beyond the project's lifetime.
9.
The component also recognizes the importance of gender-responsive planning, aiming
to ensure that women and vulnerable groups are actively involved in the planning and
implementation of adaptation measures. The participatory approach will help to secure broad-
based community ownership of the project interventions, which is essential for the project's
s 2 u .2 c .2 c . ess Caonmd cpoonnteinnuti 2ty:. Strengthening Ecosystem Resilience and Climate-Adaptive
Livelihoods
Outcome 2: Communities in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania
have increased livelihood and water security as a result of investment in EbA measures, water
access, and sustainable land and natural resources management practices (total cost: USD 28
million; GCF cost: USD 25.6 million)
10.
Component 2 of the proposed project aims to significantly enhance the resilience of
communities and ecosystems in four rural-urban hubs along the Sahara-Sahel boundary of
Mauritania. It focuses on EbA measures and other sustainable natural resource management
pr actices to address desertification and improve livelihoods in vulnerable communities.
11.
One of the core outputs under this component is establishing ~2,123 ha of green-grey
dune-stabilization infrastructure and 120 kilometres of protective fencing around dune-fixation
sites. These interventions will stabilize sand dunes and restore critical ecosystem services like
soil stabilization and water infiltration, which will significantly reduce the impacts of
desertification and enhance local biodiversity. These efforts will help mitigate sand
encroachment on infrastructure and agriculture, leading to increased resilience and economic
op portunities for local populations.
12.
In addition to dune stabilization, the component focuses on improving water access for
agricultural and land rehabilitation activities. This will be achieved by installing improved water
management infrastructure, including rainwater harvesting systems and cisterns, enhancing
water availability for both agricultural use and household consumption. This infrastructure will
directly benefit communities by improving food and water security, which is crucial for their
res ilience against climate change.
13.
The component will also support climate-resilient agricultural livelihoods by
introducing sustainable land and natural resource-use practices. This includes training local
farmers on climate-smart agriculture and alternative livelihoods such as beekeeping and
poultry farming. The project will also assist in market access and develop value chains to help
far mers diversify income sources, reducing their dependency on fragile ecosystems.
14.
A Small Grants Facility (SGF) will be established to provide continued financial support
for scaling up successful EbA interventions and sustainable livelihoods, fostering community-
Project management (total cost: USD 1.7 million; GCF cost: USD 1.5 million)
led initiatives and enhancing long-term resilience in the target hubs.
15.
The project will be executed by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable
Development (MEDD) in Mauritania, which will be supported by the United Nations
Environment Programme (UNEP) as the accredited entity (AE). UNEP will oversee the entire
project, ensuring that it aligns with GCF standards. As part of this arrangement, UNEP will also
be responsible for managing the project's financial oversight, reporting and ensuring
compliance with both GCF and UNEP policies.

=== PAGE 79 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 79
16.
A Project Steering Committee (PSC) will provide strategic direction, consisting of
representatives from MEDD, key ministries, and local authorities. A project management unit
(PMU) within MEDD, led by a Project Coordinator, will handle daily operations and reporting.
The PMU will include specialists in financial management, procurement, gender, safeguards and
mo nitoring. A Technical Committee, chaired by MEDD, will provide technical guidance.
17.
In addition, implementing partners, such as local non-governmental organizations like
Naforé, will be engaged for on-the-ground interventions in target hubs. Naforé will assist with
ecosystem restoration, livelihood improvement, and water infrastructure activities. Other
pa rtners may be onboarded as the project progresses to enhance local implementation.
18.
The GCF portion of this project management cost is less than 5 per cent of the total
Monitoring and evaluation component (total cost: USD 1.8 million; GCF cost: USD 1.8 million)
requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF Policy on Fees.
19.
The project's monitoring, reporting, and evaluation arrangements will be coordinated
by a full-time Monitoring Officer, responsible for designing a performance monitoring
framework. This framework will track progress against project indicators and ensure regular
reporting to the PSC and the PMU. Gender-disaggregated indicators will also be monitored to
en sure gender targets are met.
20.
Key evaluation activities include an interim and a final evaluation. The interim
evaluation, managed by UNEP, will assess the project's performance at its midpoint, addressing
challenges and recommending corrective actions. The final evaluation, conducted by an
independent consultant overseen by the UNEP Evaluation Office, will assess the overall project
impact based on established evaluation criteria, including relevance, effectiveness and
sustainability. These evaluations will be shared with stakeholders for feedback before public
dis closure.
21.
Furthermore, the project will produce annual progress reports and semi-annual
financial reports. UNEP will manage these reports, ensuring they meet GCF standards. Field
visits and supervision will be conducted by UNEP to ensure continuous monitoring and address
any implementation challenges.
III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria
Scale: N/A
3. 1 Impact potential
22.
The adaptation impact potential of the project is considerable, focusing on enhancing
the resilience of communities and ecosystems along the Sahara-Sahel boundary in Mauritania.
The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 individuals by providing improved protection
against sand inundation, enhancing access to markets, income-generating opportunities, and
clean water. Additionally, it will indirectly benefit 145,008 people through ecosystem
restoration, better water management infrastructure, and strengthened climate adaptation
capacity. The project aims to rehabilitate 2,123 ha of degraded land, enhance water security
through improved infrastructure, and promote climate-resilient agriculture, with positive
spillover effects across surrounding regions. This comprehensive approach will help increase
resilience for the entire population in the four target hubs, benefiting approximately 230,237
people in total.
Scale: N/A
3.2 Paradigm shift potential

=== PAGE 80 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 80
23.
The project’s paradigm shift potential is considerable as it aims to create systemic
changes in local climate adaptation practices through the establishment of coordination
platforms and the integration of EbA into national and local policies. By creating these platforms
and promoting knowledge-sharing, the project enhances the capacity of local and regional
stakeholders to manage climate risks and implement climate-resilient land and water
management practices. This can lead to the institutionalization of EbA approaches at the local
lev el and create a foundation for long-term climate resilience.
24.
However, the scalability of these solutions beyond the four target regions is largely
dependent on broader institutional uptake and the availability of funding. While the project can
serve as a demonstration model for future initiatives, its success in catalysing systemic change
across Mauritania depends on the ability to secure continued financial support and the political
will to integrate EbA into national policies. The wider replication of these practices will require
strong engagement from national and regional institutions to embed the project’s outcomes into
broader policy frameworks and funding mechanisms.
Scale: N/A
3. 3 Sustainable development potential
25.
The project demonstrates strong sustainable development potential, providing multiple
co-benefits that align with the GCF Investment Criteria and contribute to several Sustainable
Development Goals (SDGs). The project will enhance biodiversity through ecosystem
restoration activities and will also help to conserve biodiversity by mitigating desertification
and preventing further ecosystem degradation. This aligns with SDG 15 (life on land), which
foc uses on protecting, restoring and promoting the sustainable use of terrestrial ecosystems.
26.
The project will also have a direct impact on food security by promoting climate-
resilient agriculture and livelihood diversification. The project will strengthen local food
production by training farmers in climate-smart agricultural practices and providing improved
irrigation systems, thereby reducing vulnerability to climate shocks and enhancing SDG 2 (zero
hunger). Additionally, the establishment of alternative livelihoods such as beekeeping and
poultry farming will provide communities with diversified income sources, ensuring food
av ailability even during challenging climatic conditions.
27.
Furthermore, improved community health is a key outcome of the project. The
restoration of ecosystems and improved water management infrastructure will enhance water
quality and availability, reducing waterborne diseases and improving overall sanitation. Access
to clean water, especially through communal water points and rainwater harvesting systems,
will directly support SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation). The project also seeks to reduce health
risks through better infrastructure protection from sand encroachment, which will safeguard
vulnerable communities from respiratory diseases caused by dust storms and improve the
ge neral well-being of the population.
28.
Additionally, the project emphasizes gender equality by ensuring that women are
actively engaged in the planning, decision-making and implementation of climate adaptation
interventions. This supports SDG 5 (gender equality), as women’s participation in leadership
roles and sustainable livelihood initiatives helps to empower them economically and socially,
while also contributing to broader community development.
Scale: N/A
3. 4 Needs of the recipient
29.
The project effectively responds to the most pressing challenges for Mauritania by
addressing the country’s high vulnerability to climate change impacts and the challenges related
to desertification, water scarcity and economic development. As one of the most vulnerable
countries globally (twelfth on the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative Vulnerability Index)

=== PAGE 81 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 81
and with relatively low readiness for climate action (118th on the Readiness Index), Mauritania
fac es significant barriers to climate adaptation.
30.
The project tackles desertification directly by implementing EbA measures, such as dune
fixation and ecosystem restoration, across 2,123 ha. This addresses one of the country’s most
pressing environmental challenges, aiming to slow the spread of the desert into fertile
ag ricultural areas and protecting livelihoods from sand encroachment.
31.
Water scarcity is another critical issue in Mauritania. The project improves water
security by installing water management infrastructure, including groundwater recharge dams,
solar-powered boreholes, and rainwater harvesting systems. Additionally, the project responds
to the country’s low economic development by promoting climate-resilient livelihoods through
sustainable agriculture, livelihood diversification (such as beekeeping and poultry farming), and
strengthening food security.
Scale: N/A
3. 5 Country ownership
32.
The project demonstrates strong country ownership by aligning closely with the
national adaptation priorities of Mauritania and has been designed in collaboration with
stakeholders at all levels. It directly addresses several key objectives from first nationally
determined contribution of Mauritania, particularly in areas such as restoring natural
grasslands, conducting hydrological assessments, strengthening ecosystem resilience, and
enhancing the resilience of vulnerable populations, especially in rural regions. The project also
contributes to the country’s national adaptation programme of action (NAPA), which
encourages a cross-sectoral approach to climate adaptation. It directly addresses 19 of the 27
priority adaptation measures outlined in the NAPA, including EbA interventions, water
ma nagement techniques, and capacity-building for communities affected by climate change.
33.
The project also supports the ongoing national adaptation plan development process in
Mauritania, which began in 2018, by enhancing the capacity of both policymakers and
vulnerable groups. This will include activities such as awareness-raising campaigns targeting
local authorities, and training programmes for relevant government staff. Additionally, the
project aligns with the country’s spatial planning strategy, which uses an oasis-centred
ad ministrative structure to enhance resilience to desertification and mitigate rural exodus.
34.
Extensive consultations with a wide range of stakeholders – including government
representatives, civil society organizations, the private sector and local communities – have
been conducted to ensure the project meets the country’s adaptation needs. As the national
designated authority, MEDD has been actively involved since the inception of the project,
contributing to the design process and validating the proposed interventions. Over 330 local
community members from the target hubs participated in consultations, ensuring the project
reflects the needs and priorities of the people of Mauritania.
Scale: N/A
3. 6 Efficiency and effectiveness
35.
The proposal presents a robust and context-specific economic and financial analysis
(EFA), with clearly delineated methodologies for monetizing ecosystem services and estimating
household-level returns. The EFA captures both market and non-market benefits across
agricultural production, livestock management, water access and health system cost savings.
The economic analysis utilizes an ecosystem service indexing approach, combined with
macroeconomic contribution analysis (including multiplier effects and health-cost savings). For
the financial analysis, a household-level analysis simulates with/without project scenarios,
integrating productivity gains expected through GCF-financed interventions. The structure is
consistent with GCF EFA guidance, and sensitivity analysis is included.

=== PAGE 82 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 82
36.
Long-term economic viability is supported by positive economic indicators. The
economic analysis estimates a net present value of USD 151.5 million over a 20-year horizon,
against project costs of USD 31.2 million. This yields a benefit-to-cost ratio of 5.86 and an
economic internal rate of return (IRR) of 29 per cent. The analysis uses a 2 per cent social
discount rate, lower than the GCF standard benchmark of 5–10 per cent, but justified given the
non-market nature of ecosystem services and the strong resilience co-benefits delivered. The
benefits encompass improved productivity in crop and livestock systems, enhanced access to
water, economy-wide multipliers, and monetized health-cost savings from reduced
ma lnutrition.
37.
The financial analysis presents beneficiary-level cash flows and returns under “without”
and “with” GCF support scenarios for oasis-based agriculture and livestock systems. With GCF
interventions, the financial IRR improves from 12 per cent to 22 per cent (with a financial net
1
present value of USD 42.6 million), indicating enhanced viability at household level. However,
the analysis shows that most financial value accrues via subsistence consumption, limiting
direct revenue generation. The EFA also incorporates a qualitative justification for 100 per cent
grant support, citing multidimensional poverty, limited surplus income, low private sector
participation, and fiscal constraints. The SGF is mentioned in the analysis, but it was not
modelled in the financial analysis. Further development of activity-specific financial models for
SGF-supported interventions would enhance the robustness and credibility of the analysis,
particularly in demonstrating the financial viability and sustainability of these subprojects from
a b eneficiary perspective.
38.
The adaptation cost per direct beneficiary is estimated at USD 362.6, and USD 134.2 per
total beneficiary. These figures are above the GCF portfolio averages for Africa, which are
usually under USD 200 per direct beneficiary. While the cost per beneficiary is relatively high,
this is explained by the project’s focus on foundational EbA measures, and the inclusion of
public goods such as water access and land restoration. The EFA demonstrates strong value for
money by linking these cost levels to substantial economic benefits – with an estimated benefit-
to-cost ratio of 5.86 and an economic IRR of 29 per cent. Overall, the economic and financial
metrics reinforce the justification for full grant financing in the context of a least developed
country.

(ESP). The project has been classified as Category B, based the outcome of risk screening
against the ESS standards that showed moderate to low risks. The environmental and social
risk categorization is consistent with that of the revised ESP. Based on the on the
environmental and social risk screening undertaken by the AE, potential risks are likely to be

1
The financial discount rate of 12 per cent provided in the assumptions sheet serves as a benchmark for evaluating
financial viability but is not directly applied in the financial IRR calculation. Financial IRR is computed
independently of any discount rate, based solely on projected cash flows. Furthermore, the baseline cost structure is

not align with standard financial analysis practice, which requires costs to be independently estimated from actual
or benchmarked inputs. As a result, both baseline and project IRR figures should be interpreted with caution.

S ocial Standard 7
(Indigenous Peoples
44.
). The AE has undertaken an assessment against the Indigenous Peoples
Policy and engaged with authorities and civil society organizations to determine the presence of
Indigenous Peoples within the project area, and confirms that none have been identified. The
project is committed to inclusive and culturally appropriate engagement with all vulnerable and
mobile groups through broader environmental and social safeguards and will establish a
community-based coordination mechanism to facilitate management of resources. The project
further indicates that, should Indigenous Peoples be identified during project implementation,
an Indigenous Peoples Plan or an Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework will be developed.

GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 84
Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment
45.
. The revised GCF
Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM-2021/18 requires safeguarding
from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. The AE
provided SEAH safeguarding in its submission to this funding proposal. The project ESMP
identifies SEAH as one of the social risks and highlights key measures to address SEAH and
gender-based violence (GBV) in alignment with the AE’s approach to SEAH and/or its
equivalence. The risks in relation to SEAH have been identified from: (i) the outcomes of the
UNEP risk screening; (ii) consideration of local legal requirements; and (iii) general good
practice principles in the context of the proposed project interventions. Both annexes 6 and 8
have ensured that the approach to SEAH is survivor-centred. The AE noted that because of the
absence of a legal framework for GBV in the country, several limitations affecting the GBV and
SEAH grievance redress mechanisms have been identified. These include: (i) weak protection
and accountability structures; (ii) limited standardized reporting mechanisms; and (iii) limited
coordination with law enforcement. The AE has therefore put in place a SEAH management
process (SEAH 9-step procedure) to ensure the safety of the survivors and continued
psychosocial support services. In addition to this, the project will allocate SEAH focal points as
well as the development of indicators to track the effectiveness of SEAH/GBV prevention and
reporting systems. The PMU will comprise a gender officer to oversee any grievances related to
GBV or SEAH. In addition, gender-related training will be provided to the PMU and include
topics such as understanding and preventing SEAH and GBV.
4. 2 Gender policy
46.
The UNEP gender assessment for this project highlights the various challenges women
and marginalized groups face, especially in the four regional hubs of northern Mauritania. The
assessment describes the limiting factors for women including technical skills and access to
means of production, such as land and credit, as well as gender norms and perceptions. The
assessment describes potential risks for the participation of women in the project. Key gender
project management outputs have been categorized as per each project outcome area. The
project will ensure that there is a gender balance when establishing the PMU and management
of its human resources. A gender officer will also be recruited in the PMU to support and
monitor the effective implementation of the Gender Action Plan and oversee grievances related
to SEAH/GBV.
47.
Activities to be implemented in this project include: gender training for the PMU;
facilitating the participation of women in the project monitoring and learning; building women’s
leadership and agency; ensuring that national policies, plans and strategies on natural resource
management and land-use planning are gender-responsive; developing knowledge products on
project lessons learned and best practices through a participatory process that engages the
communities; and demonstrating gender-responsive best practices in adaptation planning. The
project will further document case studies featuring women's leadership in EbA interventions;
provide gender-sensitive technical training on dune-fixation techniques; ensure at least 30 per
cent female representation in water user groups; develop gender-sensitive water regulation
plans that consider women’s water needs for domestic and agricultural use; ensure women’s
participation in selecting locations for rainwater-harvesting systems and communal cisterns;
and prioritize access to harvested rainwater for women-led smallholder farms and household
use. It has been recommended that the AE should make any relevant mid-course corrections as
and when applicable during implementation of the Gender Action Plan and leverage other
opportunities where the gender targets, especially for women, could be increased given the
social and cultural context. The Gender Action Plan consists of activities, performance
indicators, baselines, targets, means of verification, budget, timelines and responsibilities.

50.
Implementation challenges: The four target regions where the project will be
implemented – Aoujeft, Rachid, Tamcheket and Nema – are not immediately adjacent but are
within a few hundred kilometres of each other. The distance between these regions may present
logistical challenges that could affect the efficiency and timeliness of implementation. To
mitigate this, the project budget accounts for the logistical demands of coordinating activities
acr oss multiple sites, including transportation and communication.
51.
Lack of buy-in: There is a risk of resistance to the project activities due to a lack of
understanding of the benefits or vested interests conflicting with the project goals. While
consultations have been conducted to encourage community buy-in, ongoing engagement
through awareness campaigns is necessary to address this challenge. A participatory approach
will be integral to overcoming such challenges, ensuring that local communities take ownership
of the project and are committed to its long-term success.
52.
Co-finance: Although co-financing from the government accounts for only 8 per cent of
the total project financing and is in-kind in the form of staff time as well as office facilities to
support the operations of the PMU, the government has committed USD 1.5 million for specific
project activities, specifically to provide inputs required to cover 905 ha (42 per cent) of the
total 2123 ha of dune stabilization to be undertaken. If the co-financing does not materialize
fully, it could lead to delays and/or reduced scope of activities. The AE has secured co-financing
commitment letters from the government, and it will be required to report to GCF on co-

4. 4 Fiduciary
58.
The project will be implemented by the United Nations Environment Programme
(UNEP), as the accredited entity (AE), in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and
Sustainable Development (MEDD) of Mauritania, which serves as the executing entity (EE).
UNEP will retain overall responsibility for project supervision, fiduciary compliance, financial
oversight, and reporting to GCF. MEDD will be responsible for executing day-to-day project
activities and managing country-level implementation in accordance with agreed plans. A
de dicated Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established to coordinate project execution.
59.
Oversight and strategic direction will be provided by a PSC composed of relevant
government institutions, including the Ministries of Finance, Health, and Agriculture. A
representative of UNEP will also form part of the PSC. This committee will have a decision-
making capacity and will primarily serve to provide project oversight and advisory support,
including: a) overseeing project implementation; and b) reviewing annual budget and
wo rkplans.
60.
All financial management will be governed by the financial regulations and rules of
UNEP. UNEP will manage GCF proceeds through a dedicated grant within its trust fund
structure, and expenditures will be tracked through the UNEP enterprise resource planning
system using a work breakdown structure. MEDD will submit quarterly financial requests for
the activities under its responsibility, which will be reviewed and approved by UNEP. Financial
reporting will follow international standards and UNEP policies, and will be supported by

4. 5 Results monitoring and reporting
63.
The project will contribute to all adaptation results areas largely towards increased
resilience of most vulnerable people and communities (35 per cent) and of ecosystems and
ecosystem services (35 per cent), and partly towards health and well-being, and food and water
security (15 per cent) and infrastructure and built environment (15 per cent). These results
areas are represented in the theory of change where appropriate assumptions were also
art iculated.
64.
A logical framework was developed to include indicators that relate to these results
areas and as consistent with the GCF Integrated Results Framework and draft Results
Handbook. Specifically, the proposed project will monitor and report progress against: Core
Indicator 2: Direct and Indirect Beneficiaries Reached; Supplementary 2.1: Beneficiaries
(female/male) adopting improved and/or new climate-resilient livelihood options;
Supplementary 2.5: Beneficiaries (female/male) adopting innovations that strengthen climate
change resilience; Supplementary 2.3: Beneficiaries (female/male) with more climate-resilient
water security; Core 4: Hectares of natural resources brought under improved low-emission
and/or climate-resilient management practice; Supplementary 4.1: Hectares of terrestrial
forest, terrestrial non-forest, fresh-water and coastal marine areas brought under restoration
and/or improved ecosystems; and Supplementary 4.2: Number of livestock brought under
su stainable management practices.
65.
Baseline data and mid-line and endline targets have been specified for all the above
indicators. The means of verification combines primary and secondary data sources including
those collected through household surveys, geographic information systems mapping,
government data, interviews and focus group discussions. With a budget of USD 1.8 million for
monitoring and evaluation, the proposal provides confidence that data collection for monitoring
and reporting purposes would be sufficient and robust. Thus, based on the assessments, the
proposed project has provided for the required resources to ensure compliance with the
requirements of the monitoring and accountability framework, Integrated Results Management
Framework and the Evaluation Policy.

=== PAGE 88 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 88
4. 6 Legal assessment
66.
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15
December 2016, and became effective on 20 February 2017, which was amended and restated
pursuant to a first amendment and restatement agreement dated 13 July 2023, and which
be came effective on 2 August 2023 (the “AMA”)
67.
The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all
int ernal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.
68.
The proposed project will be implemented in the Islamic Republic of Mauritania,
country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst
other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks
need to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of GCF to undertake redress activities and/or
investigations in the country may be hindered due to the absence of privileges and immunities
for relevant GCF personnel.
69.
Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF
proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation
and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and
im munities for GCF and its personnel.
70.
To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by
the Board is made subject to the following conditions:
(a)
Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the
GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
(b)
Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of
FP266
Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of ecosystems and populations in
four regional hubs in northern Mauritania
Accredited entity: United Nations Environmental Programme (UNEP)
Country: Mauritania
Project size: Small
I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
1.1 Overview
1
1.
This funding proposal for a small-sized adaptation project in environmental and social
safeguards category B is submitted by the Government of Mauritania (GoM) through the United
Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) as the proposed project’s accredited entity (AE). The
executing entity (EE) is the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) of
Mauritania. The total cost of the funding proposal is USD 33.56 million, of which USD 30.90
million is proposed as GCF finance and USD 2.66 million as in-kind co-finance from the GoM. The
project implementation period is six years, while benefits are calculated over a lifespan of 20
years.
2.
The proposed project’s objective is to enhance the resilience of communities and
ecosystems against climate change-induced desertification in the area of the Sahara–Sahel
boundary within Mauritania. The project will focus on four rural–urban hubs (see the figure):
Aoujeft (in the Adrar wilayah, or region), Rachid (in the Tagant wilayah), Tamchekett (in the
Hodh El Gharbi wilayah) and Nema (in the Hodh Chargui wilayah). The project is designed to
help vulnerable communities and their settlements and livelihoods to adapt to climate change-
intensified desert encroachment, sandstorms, heat, drought and flood, with a particular focus on

Page 91
Climate change risk and impacts.
5.
Observations of historical climate trends show that
the mean annual temperature in Mauritania has increased by 0.3 °C per decade over the past 70
2
years – from 26.10 °C in 1950 to 28.46 °C in 2020 . In addition to shifting temperatures, climate
change in Mauritania has led to more frequent extreme rainfall events over short time periods
(with associated flash floods, and a decline in the kind of low-intensity, long-duration rainfall
3
that infiltrates into the ground), increased rainfall irregularity and more prolonged dry spells.
Precipitation has decreased during the wet season and over the entire year in Rachid,
Tamchekett and Nema. In contrast, the Aoujeft hub has experienced a small but statistically
significant increase in its overall precipitation. All four hubs have seen an increase in extreme
daily rainfall, which often results in flash floods. The observed potential evapotranspiration
(ETP) values are high in all four hubs (1,700–2,000 mm/year), with negative differences
between precipitation and ETP, indicating that water loss via ETP heaxrcmeeadtsta wnater gained via
precipitation. Sand movements are driven by the prevalence of strong winds from the north of
Mauritania, including both the north-eastern trade winds and the – a dry wind
strongest from late November to mid-March. Together, these winds carry large amounts of dust
an d sand across the country.
6.
In future, all four target hubs are projected to experience increases in both minimum
and maximum temperatures under future Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5
(intermediate) and RCP 8.5 (high) greenhouse gas emissions scenarios, with frequency of
4
heatwaves predicted to increase across all four hubs by 2050 under both scenarios. The
projected evolution of precipitation trends varies between the hubs. No significant change in
precipitation is projected for Aoujeft by 2050 under either scenario, but annual precipitation is
expected to significantly decrease in Rachid, Tamchekett and Nema, relative to the reference
period (1981–2010), under both scenarios. In line with increasing temperature trends and
decreasing precipitatiopn trends, potential ETP is projected to increase across all four hubs by
2050 under both scenarios, and the water balance deficit in all four hubs is also projected to
5
in crease significantly ( < 0.1) by 2050.
7.
The combination of projected temperature rise, ETP and declining rainfall will increase
the extent and severity of droughts at the national and local levels. By 2040, the median number
of consecutive dry days for Mauritania is predicted to be between 219 and 230, compared to the
historical value of 216 days in 1995. The Standardised Precipitation Evapotranspiration Index
6
(SPEI) displays a clear trend towards worsening drought conditions, with values below zero by
7
2040. By 2100, the ninetieth-percentile values are expected to fall below −2, signifying an
increase in extremely dry years. In its analysis, the AE has applied an assumption that wind
conditions will remain constant under future climate scenarios in all four hubs. The
combination of constant windy conditions and a generally warmer and drier climate is likely to
facilitate the southward expansion of the Sahara into the Sahelian region of the country, further
im pacting the communities of the project’s focal hubs.
8.
Prolonged droughts across the country have also accelerated vegetation loss in desert
environments, reducing ecosystem services related to soil stabilization and water infiltration. As
a result, the rate of dune advancement along the Sahara–Sahel boundary has increased and
greater sand encroachment has been observed in oases, with subsequent impacts on water
resources, agricultural productivity and income generation across northern Mauritania. Arable
land, rangeland and pastures are being inundated, as is infrastructure such as roads, schools
2
3
Unless stated otherwise, all sources for statistics are shown in the funding proposal.
See table 32 in annex 2 of the funding proposal for observed precipitation trends in the four focal hubs.
4
See annex 2 of the funding proposal, appendix 2: “Trend analysis report for Mauritania”.
5
See annex 2 of the funding proposal, appendix 4: “Climate change risk assessment for Mauritania”.
6
The SPEI is an indicator of drought severity that accounts for precipitation and potential evapotranspiration. Values
of +2 or higher indicate extremely wet years, while values −2 or less indicate extremely dry years.
7
Sixth Assessment Report of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change.

and hospitals, with sand sometimes covering entire villages. Siltation of dams, boreholes, wells
and rivers has reduced the accessibility and availability of already scarce water resources in the
region. This is combined with reduced rainfall and water infiltration resulting from vegetation
loss increasing the depth of the water table. This has led to many pastoral wells drying up,
impacting pastoralists’ abilities to maintain nomadic lifestyles. Consequently, many nomadic
pastoralists are adopting sedentary lifestyles around rural oases, increasing the pressure on

9.
The adaptation impact potential of the project is considerable,
focusing on enhancing the resilience of communities living in and around oases in the
vulnerable interface between the arid Sahel zone and the hyperarid Sahara Desert to drought,
flood and desert encroachment. Through Output 2.1, the exposure of urban and rural
communities in the target hubs to sand inundation, escalated by climate change, will be reduced.
This will be achieved through enhanced ecosystem services generated by physical dune fixation
using a wattling technique to create a stabilized grid, fenced in to allow full establishment, and
then mulching and vegetating these dunes with grasses, shrubs and trees to create permanent
buffers against sand. These measures will reduce the vulnerability of water sources, agricultural
production and human respiratory health to sand encroachment and dust storms. A set of grey-
green adaptation measures will be used to reduce vulnerability to flood and drought, installing
(a) groundwater dams, to improve aquifer recharge; (b) gabions, dykes and stone bunds, to
reduce flood risk; and (c) reservoirs, boreholes, well points and rainwater-harvesting systems,
to increase rainwater capture and storage. This will be complemented through Output 2.2,
which will provide (a) training and inputs for climate-resilient crop agriculture garnadr ansat ural
resource-based livelihoods; (b) training on climate-resilient and sustainable livestock
8
management practices; (c) communal boreholes equipped with solar pumps in and
along historical transhumance routes, as well as rainwater-harvesting tanks; and (d) support to
community-managed water user groups in the form of commune-level (see paragraph 10
be low) water management plans.
10.
The project is expected to directly benefit 85,229 people (40,877 men and 44,352
women) living within 12 priority communes across the four target hub s, who will directly
experience improved protection against sand inundation, enhanced access to markets and
income-generating opportunities, and increased access to clean water.Additionally, the AE
estimates that there will be about 145,008 indirect beneficiaries (66,519 men and 78,489
women), representing individuals in surrounding or non-priority communes who will
experience knowledge-sharing benefits, improved coordination at the regional level, and access
to the small grants facility for scaling climate change adaptation (CCA) interventions (around 3
per cent of the country’s total population and 12 per cent of the target wilayahs). The project
aims to rehabilitate 2,123 hectares of degraded land, enhance water security through improved
infrastructure and promote climate-resilient agriculture. This comprehensive approach will
help to increase the resilience of the entire population in the four target hubs, benefiting

14.
The iTAP notes that the AE used a soil and water assessment tool
(SWAT+) for hydrological modelling to evaluate the potential impacts of water resource
management strategies by comparing a business-as-usual scenario with a scenario with
interventions (involving installation of physical structures to manage and increase water
infiltration). The analysis focused on identifying spatial patterns of run-off generation,
infiltration potential and water balance components, to generate for each potential site the
ex pected difference between scenarios, indicating where investment would be most effective.
15.
Chapter 7 of the feasibility study (annex 2) outlines the SWAT+ model set-up (inputs,
baseline and intervention runs) but fails to include calibration of the model and its validation
against measured streamflow or observed flood events. In response to a question about this, the
AE acknowledged this gap, which was a result of limited availability of continuous and reliable
discharge records at appropriate spatial resolution across the study domain. As a result, the AE
relied on spatial differentiation in run-off and evaporation regimes as proxies for identifying
hydrologically responsive sub-basins suitable for engineered or ecosystem-based interventions.
Because it was not possible to calibrate and validate the SWAT+ model against observed flows,
its outputs were not used for site selection; instead, the AE conducted a multi-criteria risk
ass essment, which was then validated with stakeholders.

The iTAP assesses the impact potential of the project as high.

18.
Numerous project studies and assessments will be
conducted within the target hubs to improve the country’s climate change knowledge base,
including assessments of value chains, surface and groundwater quality and quantity, and
community vulnerability (disaggregated by gender). All the assessments will contribute to a
CCA knowledge management platform, which will be used as a centralized repository accessible
to stakeholders across different administrative levels. Best practices on CCA will be collated,
tailored and incorporated into knowledge products, informing the design and implementation
of subsequent adaptation interventions. This approach to knowledge management is intended
to support both vertical scaling (enhancing the impact within the target hubs) and horizontal
scaling (supporting CCA in other hubs, countries or regions) of successful adaptation measures.
These knowledge products will also be integrated into the country’s national adaptation plan
(NAP) knowledge management platform, which manages knowledge related to desertification

19.
At commune level, processes will be put in place to achieve a
paradigm shift in the way resources are managed – including establishing commune-level
technical committees and water user groups, strengthening local institutional and regulatory
frameworks for monitoring water use and availability, training farmer organizations (including
women) on climate-smart agriculture, and establishing a small grants facility to recycle some of
the proceeds from sustainable livelihood activities back into adaptation beyond the project
implementation period. This new approach is not reflected at the national level, however. In the
project’s theory of change, it is suggested that climate change resilience will be enhanced “if
supported by transformational change in policies, planning, knowledge management and
cap acity-building”, but the project focus only at the local and regional levels limits such change.
20.
The AE acknowledges that the project “takes a bottom-up approach to achieving
transformational change and the integration of EbA [ecosystem-based adaptation] approaches
into national and sub-national policies” and “lays the foundation for the integration of EbA
15
approaches into wilayah-, moughataa- and commune-level development plans”. This is to be
achieved through the development and dissemination of knowledge products, including policy
briefs, but there are no planned points of formal engagement with national government and
other regional government agencies that would allow for long-term changes to policy and
financing frameworks for Mauritania as a whole, particularly for other oases and other areas
vulnerable to sand encroachment. This seems a missed opportunity for the GCF project to
contribute to transformational change by fostering long-term policy reforms in natural resource

Development, contributing to a wide range of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs),
namely: SDG 2 – Zero hunger; SDG 3 – Good health and well-being; SDG 5 – Gender equality;
SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 8 – Decent work and economic growth; SDG 10 –
Reduced inequalities; SDG 11 – Sustainable cities and communities; SDG 12 – Responsible
consumption and production; SDG 13 – Climate action; SDG 15 – Life on land; and SDG 16 –

24.
Support of climate-resilient agriculture, forest products and
agriprocessing is expected to improve households’ nutritional status, reducing dependence on
imports and, in some cases, food aid. Dune stabilization efforts should reduce the prevalence of

25.
The gender assessment and action plan (annex 8 to the
funding proposal) highlights that although women in the rural areas of Mauritania are involved
in subsistence farming, food processing and livestock management, they have limited access to
land, financial resources, training and agricultural inputs. Less than 10 per cent of women have
access to formal agricultural extension services, even though up to 50 per cent of households
are headed by women in many areas. This limits women’s capacity to improve productivity and
resilience to climate impacts. Section 1.13 of annex 8 also highlights the ongoing challenges of
social inclusion in a country with a complex past characterized by enslavement of parts of the
population. The assessment notes that “women are responsible for a considerable amount of
household work, including agricultural production, post-harvest activities, collecting fuelwood
and water, food preparation and childcare” and acknowledges the need to avoid additional
burdens on women through new climate-smart agricultural practices, sustainable livelihoods
an d water management techniques.
26.
The proposed project interventions will address gender equality through several
pathways. One will be to incorporate a gender action plan into new development plans and
community climate action plans, based on the specific gaps identified within each community.

awareness is maintained of the need to balance such new tasks by freeing up other time – for

27.
The project’s lengthy and detailed feasibility
study (annex 2) notes that groundwater extraction in oases is hindered by low levels of aquifer
recharge, with a continually falling water table; that enforcement of regulations to control water
extraction is limited; that overuse adversely affects marginalized groups, particularly women;
and that baseline assessments conducted during project development highlight the risk of
groundwater depletion when the use of solar-powered pumps is not strictly monitored. The
funding proposal includes hydrogeological studies and maps in subactivity 2.2.1.3 as the basis
for the appropriate siting of four new boreholes to be drilled per community, and the iTAP
welcomes these measures. But there is no discussion of ongoing water-level monitoring and
community governance of pumping in order to avoid unsustainable extraction, which is
nowhere treated as a project risk. The iTAP believes this is an oversight. It recommends that
this issue be included in the environmental and social management plan and that a subactivity
be added to establish a governance system to determine and enforce pumping times and
volumes, to monitor changes in the water table, and to take action to further restrict pumping if
wa ter levels fall beneath a predetermined threshold.

high.
Scale: N/A
1. 5 Needs of the recipient
29.
Mauritania is classified as a least developed country, with a gross domestic product
16
(GDP) of about USD 7.9 billion and a GDP per capita of about USD 1,700. As a result of high
poverty rates, particularly within rural areas, the population is heavily dependent on agriculture
for subsistence, with most households’ capacities to adopt new climate-resilient livelihoods
being limited. The country’s Human Capital Index score of 38, which ranks it 150th out of 157
countries, indicates that a child born in Mauritania can only expect to attain 38 per cent of
maximum potential human capital by the age of 18, even if they receive complete health and
education, with the country still in the “low human development” stage despite significant
progress over the past 30 years. In 2019, Mauritania was ranked the country twentieth most
vulnerable to climate change and other global challenges, according to the Notre Dame Global
Adaptation Initiative country index. With the highest global sensitivity score (0.602), Mauritania
fac es substantial insecurity in both the food sector and the water sector.
30.
Between 1900 and 2018, natural hazards such as droughts, disease epidemics, insect
infestations and storms (strong winds and heavy rainfall) impacted more than 11.5 million
people in Mauritania, according to the Emergency Events Database. In 2019, an estimated
33,600 Mauritanians were impacted by floods, while in 2020 approximately 609,000 people
were affected by droughts. Flooding alone caused an estimated USD 5.5–11.4 billion in damages
and losses between 2000 and 2008. Under future climate change scenarios, Mauritania is
expected to face an overall decrease in water availability of 10–15 per cent; an increase in land
degradation and erosion; continued loss of arable land through southward expansion of the
Sahara Desert; a decrease in rangeland availability for livestock; and a reduction in food
availability.
16
As mentioned previously, sources for all statistics are shown by the AE in the funding proposal.

=== PAGE 97 ===
GCF/B.42/02/Add.08
Page 97
31.
The country’s high public and external debt – estimated at 79 per cent of GDP and 68 per
cent of GDP, respectively – constrain the ability of the GoM to allocate funding from the national
budget for CCA. Moreover, limited opportunities exist for public and private sector stakeholders
to access financial resources for adaptation, which is expected to cost around USD 9.3 billion
between 2015 and 2030. Given that this cost is likely to increase considerably under future
climate change conditions, external investment is crucial to enhance the resilience of the
population of Mauritania to the impacts of climate change – which are projected to intensify in
the future – on food and water security, economic growth, and human health. The project will
effectively respond to the most pressing challenges Mauritania is facing, addressing the
country’s high vulnerability to climate change impacts and its challenges related to
de sertification, water scarcity and economic development.
32.
The vulnerability of the population of Mauritania is high because of elevated levels of
poverty and a strong dependence on climate change-sensitive sectors, including agriculture,
fisheries, mining and livestock. In addition, access to basic services and facilities is unequal
between rural and urban areas, and those attempting to transition out of poverty encounter
major obstacles. As a result, approximately 6 per cent of the population of Mauritania live below
the international poverty line (USD 1.9 per day in 2011 with purchasing power parity) and
about 28 per cent live below the national poverty line. Moreover, 70 per cent of the country’s
impoverished households live in rural areas and are mainly engaged in agriculture and
livestock-rearing. This high prevalence of poverty and limited access to basic resources means
that rural communities living within the four target hubs lack the adaptive and financial
capacity to invest in climate-resilient sustainable development and are almost entirely
de pendent on external support from both government and development partners.
33.
Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are rated as high.
Scale: N/A
1. 6 Country ownership
34.
The proposed project has been designed following extensive consultation with the GoM
and a range of stakeholders, and is aligned with the policies of Mauritania. The roles of the AE
and EE, as well as those of technical partners who will support implementation, are clearly set
